, we can accommodate all of the inputs.

Next, we consider the seed lengths and output lengths for
h_{1}
.
We choose
o_{1}=2\log(1/\varepsilon)
, and then Theorem
5
tells us that we should take
\mathrm{sd}_{1}=2t_{1}o_{1}
, which is indeed how we define it.

Finally we turn to
h_{2}
, the “outer” hash function in the small hash. We must have
t_{2}=o_{1}=2\log(1/\varepsilon)
so that the output of
h_{1}
fits as the input of
h_{2}
, and this is indeed the case. We choose
o_{2}
to be a constant,
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
, to be determined later. Finally, Theorem
4
tells us that with these choices of
t_{2},o_{2}
, we must take
\mathrm{sd}_{2}={K}_{\mathrm{hash}}(o_{2}+\log t_{2})
, which is indeed what we do.
∎

The next lemma is about when a point
p
is removed from
\mathtt{AvMPs}
.

Lemma 8
.

Suppose Alice’s depth at time
t
is
\ell_{A}(t)
, and let
p\in M_{A}
be a
j
-stable meeting point. Let
t_{p}<t
being the last time Alice reached depth
p
before time
t
. Then
p\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}(t)
if and only if there exists a time
t^{\prime}\in(t_{p},t]
such that
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})\geq p+2^{j+1}
.
The analogous statement also holds for Bob.

Proof.

Notice first that
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
is updated every time Alice updates her current depth
\ell_{A}
(either in the Computation Phase after she simulates
\Pi
, or in the Transition Phase after she jumps).

To prove the lemma, we observe that
p\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}(t)
if and only if there is some time
t^{\prime}\in(t_{p},t]
so that
p
is removed from
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at that time. Indeed, since by definition
t_{p}
is the last time that Alice simulates
p
,
p
is added to
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t_{p}
, and will not be added again until after time
t
if it is forgotten. The point
p
will be removed from
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t^{\prime}
if and only if
p\not\in M_{\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})}
, as this is the condition in
MaintainAvMPs
(Algorithm
5
).

By Lemma
2
,
p\not\in M_{\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})}
if and only if
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})\geq p+2^{j+1}
.
Thus,
p\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}(t)
if and only if there is some time
t^{\prime}\in(t_{p},t]
so that
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})\geq p+2^{j+1}
, as desired.
∎

5
Analysis

In this section we analyze our protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
, and prove Theorem
1
.
We begin in Section
5.1
with some useful Lemmas and Definitions. Then in Section
5.2
, we introduce sneaky attacks and state our main technical lemma. In Section
5.3
we define and analyze our potential function
\Phi
. In Section
5.4
we prove that our hash functions behave as expected. Finally, in Section
5.5
we put everything together and prove Theorem
1
.

5.1
Useful Lemmas and Definitions

Before we can analyze the potential function and hash functions we need a few more definitions and notations. Initially we introduce some definitions followed by key lemmas which will be helpful for our analysis.

5.1.1
A few more definitions

We start with some terminology about
hash collisions
. Informally, we say that a
small hash collision
occurs whenever the small hash function
h^{s}
suffers a collision, and a
big hash collision
occurs whenever the big hash function
h^{b}
suffers a collision. Formally, we have the following definitions.

Definition 6

(Small and big hash collisions)
.

•

Let
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
, We say that a
small hash collision has occurred in iteration
I
of Algorithm
4.2
either of the following occur:

–

There is a hash collision in line
23
. That is, at the time
t
when line
23
is executed in iteration
I
:

*

for any of the variables
\mathtt{var}
from line
22
we have that
\mathtt{var}_{A}(t)\neq\mathtt{var}_{B}(t)
;

*

Alice and Bob have the same randomness, i.e.,
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{A}(t)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{B}(t)
and
(R_{\mathrm{iter}})_{A}=(R_{\mathrm{iter}})_{B}
; and

*

Alice and Bob’s outcomes are the same, i.e.,
(H_{\mathtt{var}})_{A}(t)=(H_{\mathtt{var}})_{B}(t)
.

–

There is a hash collision between information releted to different meeting point candidates in any of lines
29
,
31
,
30
. That is, at the time
t
when any of those lines are executed:

*

Both parties have the same randomness, meaning that
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{A}(t)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{B}(t)
; and

*

there is a transition candidate for Alice with megastate
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and a transition candidate for Bob with megastate
\mathtt{p}_{B}
such that
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
(meaning there exists
\mathtt{var}\in\{\mathtt{p.v},(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter}),\mathtt{p.depth}\}
such that
\mathtt{var}_{A}\neq\mathtt{var}_{B}
but
H_{\mathtt{var}_{A}}=H_{\mathtt{var}_{B}}
).

•

We say that a
big hash collision occurs at the end of a block
B
if there is a hash collision in the big hash exchange phase of block
B
, with respect to the hash function
h^{b}
in Line
74
. That is, this occurs if Alice and Bob’s inputs to the hash are different, so

(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter})_{A}\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter})_{B};

but their random seeds are the same (
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}
) and the outcomes in line
74
are the same.

Note that in the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of each block, Alice is responsible for generating half of the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
and Bob is responsible for generating the other half. The reason for this is so that, no matter what the adversary does, the probability that the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
from a block
B
collides with the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
from a
different
block
B^{\prime}
is very small. We will work out this probability and union bound over it at the end of the argument. Until then, we make the following assumption for the rest of the analysis.

Assumption 1
.

Fix any two distinct blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
during the execution of Algorithm
4.2
. Let
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)
and
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})
be the randomness used for the big hash computation in block
B
by Alice and in block
B^{\prime}
by Bob, respectively. We assume for the analysis that

(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)\neq(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})
.

Again, we stress that this assumption is not an assumption in Theorem
1
, it is only for the purpose of analysis. We will remove this assumption in the proof of Theorem
1
by showing that it holds with high probability.

Remark 10
.

For two megastates
\mathtt{p}
and
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}
that belong to the same party, we say that
\mathtt{p}\cong\mathtt{p}^{\prime}
if the two are equal in every variable
except
possibly
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.T}
. In this case, we say that “
\mathtt{p}
is equal to
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}
”.
The reason for this is that once a new megastate
\mathtt{p}
is “initialized” in Line
50
and stored in memory, the variables
\mathtt{p.iter},\mathtt{p.v},\mathtt{p.depth}
will not change. However, during the big hash computation phase at the end of the block, the variables
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.T}
may be retro-actively updated. Thus, we say that that
\mathtt{p}
and
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}
are “equal” even if these latter variables have changed.

We will single out some iterations as being “dangerous.” We have the following definition:

Definition 7

(Dangerous iteration)
.

We say that an iteration
I\in\{1,\ldots,I_{\mathrm{total}}\}
is a
dangerous iteration
if at the beginning of iteration
I
(after
k\leftarrow k+1
in Line
15
), it is the case that
\ell^{-}>0
or
k_{A}>1
or
k_{B}>1
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose that iteration
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
is not dangerous, and suppose that the adversary does not introduce any corruptions in iteration
I
and there are no corrupted randomness. If Alice and Bob both simulate
\Pi
in iteration
I
(that is, Line
44
executes for both), then their simulation in iteration
I
is correct, and
\ell^{-}
will still be equal to zero after the Computation Phase completes.
Notice that this holds whether or not a small hash collision occurs.

Proof.

Since
I
is not a dangerous iteration, by definition
\ell^{-}=0
at the beginning of the iteration. Thus, Alice and Bob’s simulated paths
\mathcal{T}_{A}
and
\mathcal{T}_{B}
agree. Therefore, if the adversary does not introduce any corruptions during their simulation of the next
r
rounds, their paths will continue to agree. Let
\mathtt{p}_{A}
be the mega-state of Alice at the end of this iteration and define
\mathtt{p}_{B}
similarly for Bob. Given that there is no corrupted randomness during this iteration, at the end of this iteration we have that, for every
\mathtt{var}\in\{\mathtt{p.v},\mathtt{p.depth},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}\}
,
\mathtt{var}_{A}=\mathtt{var}_{B}
and we will continue to have
\ell^{-}=0
.

∎

Definition 8
.

We say a small hash collision is
dangerous
if the hash collision happens during a dangerous iteration.

Definition 9

(Corrupted Randomness)
.

•

Let
B\in[B_{\mathrm{total}}]
. We say that
block
B
has corrupted randomness
if, at the end of block
B
in Algorithm
4.2
, either
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{A}\neq(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{B}
or
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}\neq(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}
.

•

Let
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}].
We say that
iteration
I
has corrupted randomness
if, at the end of iteration
I
, either

(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{A}\neq(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s})_{B}
or
(R_{\mathrm{iter}})_{A}\neq(R_{\mathrm{iter}})_{B}
.

The next notion we need is a “shadow variable” in Algorithm
4.2
, called the
Bad Vote Count
(
\mathrm{BVC}
).

Definition 10

(Bad Vote Count (
\mathrm{BVC}
))
.

Fix a party (say, Alice) executing Algorithm
4.2
. We define a “shadow variable”
\mathrm{BVC}
as follows.

•

\mathrm{BVC}
is initialized to
0
at the beginning of Algorithm
4.2
.

•

Whenever Alice
resets votes
(Lines
51
,
58
, and
68
),
\mathrm{BVC}
also resets to
0
.

•

In Line
40
,
\mathrm{BVC}
increments by one if any of the following occur:

–

v^{(i)}
is incremented but

\mathtt{q}_{i}\notin\{\mathtt{q}_{1,B},\mathtt{q}_{2,B},\mathtt{q}_{3,B}\}

where
\mathtt{q}_{i}=\mathtt{ms}(\mathrm{MPi})
is as defined on Line
28
, and
\mathtt{q}_{j,B}
is Bob’s value of
\mathtt{q}_{j}
during this iteration.

–

v^{(i)}
is not incremented but

\mathtt{q}_{i}\in\{\mathtt{q}_{1,B},\mathtt{q}_{2,B},\mathtt{q}_{3,B}\}

As usual, we use
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}
to refer to Alice’s “copy” of
\mathrm{BVC}
, and
\mathrm{BVC}_{B}
to refer to Bob’s “copy.” (Here, “copy” is in quotes, because of course Alice cannot compute
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}
; it is for analysis only.)

Lemma 10
.

Let
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
. If the
\mathrm{BVC}
(for either Alice or Bob) increments during iteration
I
, then one of the following occured:

•

A small hash collision occured in iteration
I
.

•

A corruption was introduced during iteration
I
.

•

Iteration
I
had corrupted randomness.

Proof.

Fix a party, say, Alice. Suppose that the variable
\mathrm{BVC}
increments for Alice.
By definition, this means that for some
i
, either
v^{(i)}
increments when
\mathtt{q}_{i}\notin\{\mathtt{q}_{1,B},\mathtt{q}_{2,B},\mathtt{q}_{3,B}\}
, or it doesn’t increment when
\mathtt{q}_{i}\in\{\mathtt{q}_{1,B},\mathtt{q}_{2,B},\mathtt{q}_{3,B}\}
.

In Algorithm
4.2
,
v^{(i)}
only increments if there is some index
j\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
H_{\texttt{q}_{i}.\texttt{v}}=H^{\prime}_{\texttt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}}
,
H_{\texttt{q}_{i}.\texttt{depth}}=H^{\prime}_{\texttt{q}_{j}.\texttt{depth}}
, and
H_{\texttt{q}_{i},b}=H^{\prime}_{\texttt{q}_{j},b}
.

If iteration
I
has corrupted randomness, we are done, so suppose that it does not.
Suppose that
\mathrm{BVC}
increments, and consider the following two scenarios.

•

Suppose that
v^{(i)}
increments, so such a
j
exists, but
\mathtt{q}_{i}\neq\mathtt{q}_{j,B}
.
Since
\mathtt{q}_{i}\neq\mathtt{q}_{j,B}
, then Alice’s
\mathtt{q}_{i}
and Bob’s
\mathtt{q}_{j,B}
differ on at least one of their three hashed attributes:
v
,

(\texttt{prev-seed},\texttt{prev-hash},{\texttt{T}},\texttt{iter}
), or
depth
. If they differ on
v
, then since
H_{\mathtt{q}_{i}.\texttt{v}}=H^{\prime}_{\mathtt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}}
, either we must have
H_{\mathtt{q}_{i}.\texttt{v}}=(H_{\mathtt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}})_{B}
, in which case there was a small hash collision (as we are assuming uncorrupted randomness in this iteration); or else
(H_{\mathtt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}})_{B}\neq H_{\mathtt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}}^{\prime}
, in which case what Alice received was different than what Bob sent, so the adversary must have introduced corruptions. The same argument holds for the other two attributes.

•

On the other hand, suppose that
v^{(i)}
does not increment, so
\mathtt{q}_{i}=\mathtt{q}_{j,B}
for some
j
, but either
H_{\mathtt{q}_{i}.\texttt{v}}\neq H^{\prime}_{\mathtt{q}_{j}.\texttt{v}}
,
H_{\texttt{q}_{i}.\texttt{depth}}\neq H^{\prime}_{\texttt{q}_{j}.\texttt{depth}}
, or
H_{\mathtt{q}_{i},b}\neq H^{\prime}_{\mathtt{q}_{i},b}
. As we are assuming that iteration
I
has uncorrupted randomness, then all hashes of attributes of
\mathtt{q}_{i}
and
\mathtt{q}_{i,B}
agree, so this implies that the adversary must have introduced a corruption.

This completes the proof.

∎

The potential function.

Now we can define the potential function
\Phi
that we will use to track Alice and Bob’s progress through the protocol.

Recall that for a variable
x
appearing in Algorithm
4.2
,
x_{A}
refers to Alice’s copy,
x_{B}
refers to Bob’s copy, and
x_{AB}=x_{A}+x_{B}
. Recall that the definitions of
\ell^{+},\ell^{-},
and
L^{-}
are given in Section
3
.
Define:

\Phi=\begin{cases}\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}+C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{5}E_{AB}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}=k_{B}\\
\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}k_{AB}+C_{4}E_{AB}-C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}\neq k_{B}\end{cases}

(2)

where
C_{1},\ldots,C_{6}
are constants that will be chosen later. We note that this is very similar to the potential function that was studied in the paper
[
Hae14
]
, and is the same as that in manuscript
[
HR18
]
.

As per our conventions in Section
3.4
,
for a time
t
, we use
\Phi(t)
denote the value of
\Phi
at time
t
. For an iteration
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
, we use
\Phi(I)
to denote the value of
\Phi
at the
end
of iteration
I
(in Line
68
of Algorithm
4.2
).

5.1.2
Useful lemmas

In this section, we prove a few lemmas that will be useful in our analysis of sneaky attacks and the potential function.

Our first lemma shows that our implementation of the “hash chaining” strategy is effective. In particular, Lemma
13
below shows that, assuming no big hash collisions ever occur, the values of
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
,
\mathtt{p.T}
, and
\mathtt{p.iter}
in Algorithm
4.2
will pick up on any discrepancies between Alice and Bob’s simulated paths. Before we state Lemma
13
, we state and prove two claims that will help us in the proof of Lemma
13
.

Claim 11
.

Suppose there are no big hash collisions throughout the entire protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
. Fix any block
B
, let
t_{1}
be the time just before the big hash computation phase starts in line
74
, and let
t_{2}
be the time at the end of the block
B
. Consider any two megastates
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}
from Alice and Bob’s memory, respectively. Suppose that

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})(t_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})(t_{1})

Then

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})(t_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})(t_{2}).

Proof.

First notice that big hash computation during the end of the block does not modify variable
\mathtt{p.iter}
in either mega-state. As a result, if
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{1})\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{1})
then it immediately follows that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{2})\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{2})
, and we are done.

Now assume that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{1})=\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t_{1})=I
. Then, we have two scenarios. Either
I\in B
or
I\notin B
; we consider each case below.

If
I\notin B
, then none of the variables within the mega-states gets updated during the big hash computation. Hence
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}(t_{1})=\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}(t_{2})
. Similarly for Bob,
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}(t_{1})=\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}(t_{2})
and the claim follows.

If
I\in B
, as the tuples do not match at time
t_{1}
then there must be a variable

\mathtt{var}\in\{\operatorname{\mathtt{prev-hash},\mathtt{prev-seed},\mathtt{T}}\}

such that
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}.\mathtt{var}(t_{1})\neq\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}.\mathtt{var}(t_{1})
. If the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}
is corrupted then at time
t_{2}
, then by definition
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}(t_{2})\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}(t_{2})
, and we are done. On the other hand, if
R_{\mathrm{block}}
is not corrupted then
\mathtt{var}
is an input to the big hash function, and assuming there are no hash collisions we can conclude that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}}(t_{2})\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}}(t_{2})
which completes the proof.
∎

Claim 12
.

Assume that there are no big hash collisions throughout the protocol. For any time
t
, and any two mega-states
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}
from Alice and Bob’s memory at time
t
, if
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t)\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}(t)
then there exists a variable
\mathtt{var}\in\{\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.T}\}
such that
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}.\mathtt{var}(t)\neq\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}.\mathtt{var}(t)
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, all variables are referenced at time
t
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}>\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
.

Then we have two cases:

Case 1: At time
t
, at least one of
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
or
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
is not equal to
\bot
.

Then we claim that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
. Notice that at time
t
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}\neq\bot
. Indeed, given our assumption that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}>\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
, if
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}=\bot
then
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}=\bot
, and we are assuming that they are not both
\bot
. Moreover,
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
includes
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
in the last piece of the partial simulated path that stored in
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
. On the other hand,
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
is either
\bot
or includes
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
in the last piece of simulated path stored in
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
. So we can conclude that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}
, establishing the claim.

Case 2: At time
t
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}=\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}=\bot
.

Then
t
must be after Alice computes the big hash values at the end of the block corresponding to
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
.
We claim that
(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}})\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}).

Indeed, when Alice computes the big hash value for
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{A}
, her input is
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
, while Bob’s input is
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
when he computes the big hash value for
\mathtt{p}^{*}_{B}
. Hence, Alice and Bob have different inputs to the big hash function. If
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
are in different blocks then, by Assumption
1
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}.

On the other hand, if iteration
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
is within the same block as iteration
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
, then either
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is corrupted in line
70
of Algorithm
4.2
, or it is not. If it is corrupted, then

\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},

establishing the claim. On the other hand, if
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted then, as we are assuming there are no big hash collisions, we can conclude that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}}
since Alice and Bob have different inputs for the big hash function. This completes the proof.
∎

Now we can state and prove Lemma
13
, which informally says that if there is a corruption—that is, if Alice and Bob’s simulated paths do not match—then the big hash will eventually catch it.

Lemma 13

(The big hash catches all corruptions)
.

Suppose there are no big hash collisions throughout the entire protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
.
Fix any time
t
, and let
q_{A},q_{B}
be points so that
1\leq q_{A}\leq\ell_{A}(t)
and
1\leq q_{B}\leq\ell_{B}(t)
.
Define

\mathtt{p}_{A}^{*}=\mathtt{ms}(q_{A})_{A}(t)\qquad\mathtt{p}_{B}^{*}=\mathtt{ms}(q_{B})_{B}(t).

That is,
\mathtt{p}_{A}^{*}
is the Alice’s megastate corresponding to depth
q_{A}
at time
t
.
13
13

13

Notice that Alice may not have
\mathtt{p}_{A}^{*}
in memory at time
t
;
\mathtt{p}_{A}^{*}
is just the megastate at depth
q_{A}
that Alice most recently computed before time
t
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq q_{A})}\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq q_{B})}.

Then

(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}).

Proof.

We begin with some useful notation.
Recall that the simulated paths
\mathcal{T}_{A}:=\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq q_{A})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{B}:=\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq q_{B})}
store both the simulated transcripts, as well as the iteration numbers in
\Pi^{\prime}
where each chunk of transcript was simulated. Define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}
as the set of iteration numbers included in the simulated path
\mathcal{T}
.
Define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cup\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
.

With this notation, we have the following claim.

Claim 14
.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
.
Let

I_{A}=\max\{I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\,:\,I^{\prime}<I\}

be the last iteration in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
before
I
where Alice simulated
\Pi
. Let
t_{\mathrm{cmp}}
be the time at the beginning of the computation phase of iteration
I
. Then, using the notation from Remark
10
, we have

\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}}){\ \cong\ }\mathtt{p}_{A}(I_{A}).

That is, Alice’s megastate
\mathtt{p}_{A}
at time
t_{\mathrm{cmp}}
is the same as her megastate at the end of iteration
I_{A}
,up to possible differences in the variables
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we focus on Alice; the case for Bob is identical.

Let
q=\mathtt{p.depth}_{A}(I)
be the point that Alice simulated in iteration
I
.
Notice that during iteration
I_{A}
, Alice simulated the point
q-1
; this is because in a simulated path, the simulated points are consecutive.

Further, we claim that
I_{A}
is the
last
iteration (either in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
or not) before time
t
, where Alice simulates the point
q-1
. If not, suppose that at some iteration
I^{\prime}>I_{A}
, Alice also simulated the point
q-1
. But this would overwrite the simulated path
\mathcal{T}_{A}
at the depth
q-1
, and
I_{A}
would not appear in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
, a contradiction.
This implies that
I_{A}
is the last iteration (either in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
or not) before iteration
I
where Alice simulates the point
q-1
.

Since Alice simulated depth
q
in iteration
I
, she must have began iteration
I
at depth
q-1
. Since
I_{A}
was the last iteration that Alice simulated depth
q-1
, the megastate that she begins iteration
I
with is the same as the megastate that she ended iteration
I_{A}
with. Since the megastate does not update until the computation phase, (except possibly the variables
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T}
that may be updated during the big hash phase at the end of the block, if
I
and
I_{A}
are in different blocks), the megastate she begins iteration
I
with is equal to her megastate at time
t_{\mathrm{cmp}}
. We conclude that
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}}){\ \cong\ }\mathtt{p}_{A}(I_{A}),

as desired.
∎

Now we continue with the proof of the lemma.
Notice that if
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}\neq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
, then we are done, so we assume that
\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}=\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}
. Thus, it suffices to show that in iteration

I^{*}:=\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}=\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}},

we have

(H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}},I_{H_{A}})(I^{*})\neq(H_{B},R_{B},T^{\leq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}},I_{H_{B}})(I^{*}),

where
(H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}}I_{H_{A}})(I)
is defined as the tuple
(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}_{A},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}_{A},\mathtt{p.T}_{A},\mathtt{p.iter}_{A})(I)
at the end of iteration
I
.

The proof proceeds by induction.
To set this up, suppose that
\mathcal{T}_{A}\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}
, and
let
I_{\mathrm{corr}}
be
the smallest
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}
such that either
I^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
, or
\sigma_{A}(I_{\mathrm{corr}})\neq\sigma_{B}(I_{\mathrm{corr}})
. (Here,
\sigma_{A}(I_{\mathrm{corr}})
denotes the transcript
\sigma
that Alice simulated in iteration
I_{\mathrm{corr}}
, and similarly for Bob.) That is,
I_{\mathrm{corr}}
is the earliest iteration of
\Pi^{\prime}
in which this simulated path encountered a problem, either because one party simulated while the other did not; or both simulated but simulated divergent paths.

We now prove by induction on
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}
that

\forall I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}\text{ such that }I_{\mathrm{corr}}\leq I,\ \ \ (H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I)\neq(H_{B},R_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I).

(3)

Base Case.

Let
I=I_{\mathrm{corr}}
. There are two cases, either
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
or it is not.

1.

In the first case,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
. Then by the definition of
I_{\mathrm{corr}}
,
\sigma_{A}(I)\neq\sigma_{B}(I)
. Then we have that
T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A}\neq T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B}
and hence,

(R_{A},H_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I)\neq(R_{B},H_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I),

establishing (
3
) for the base case of
I=I_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

2.

In the second case,
I\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
. Assume without loss of generality that
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}
, but not in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}.
This means that Bob did not simulate
\Pi
during iteration
I
, which implies that
\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}(I)\neq I
. In particular,
I_{H_{A}}\neq I_{H_{B}}
, and we again conclude that

(R_{A},H_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I)\neq(R_{B},H_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I),

establishing (
3
) for the base case of
I=I_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

Inductive Step.

Fix
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}
so that
I>I_{\mathrm{corr}}
, and assume that (
3
) holds for all
I^{\prime}<I
in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}
.

Again we have two cases, either
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
or it is not.

1.

Suppose that
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}
, let

I_{A}=\max\{I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\,:\,I^{\prime}<I\},

and define
I_{B}
similarly. That is,
I_{A}
is the iteration where Alice last simulated
\Pi
before
I
, and
I_{B}
is the iteration where Bob last simulated
\Pi
before
I
.

Let
t_{\mathrm{cmp}}
be the time at the beginning of the computation phase of iteration
I
. Then from Claim
14
we conclude that

\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})\cong\mathtt{p}_{A}(I_{A})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})\cong\mathtt{p}_{B}(I_{B}).

Now, either (a)
I_{A}\neq I_{B}
or (b)
I_{A}=I_{B}
.

Case (a)
I_{A}\neq I_{B}
.

From Claim
12
we know that there exists a variable
\mathtt{var}\in\{\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T}\}
such that
\mathtt{p}_{A}.\mathtt{var}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}.\mathtt{var}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})
.
During iteration
I
, the variables
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
do not change. As a result, if
\mathtt{var}\in\{\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}\}
, then the lemma follows. If
\mathtt{var}=\mathtt{p.T}
, then during
I
both Alice and Bob append a piece of transcript
(\sigma_{A},I)
and
(\sigma_{B},I)
to
\mathtt{p.T}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p.T}_{B}
, respectively. However if
\mathtt{p}_{A}.\mathtt{T}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}.\mathtt{T}(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})
then we have that
\mathtt{p}_{A}.\mathtt{T}\circ(\sigma_{A}\circ I)\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}.\mathtt{T}\circ(\sigma_{B}\circ I)
. Thus,
T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A}\neq T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B}
, therefore

(R_{A},H_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I)\neq(R_{B},H_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I),

as desired.

Case (b)
I_{A}=I_{B}=I^{\prime}
.

Then by induction we know that
(R_{A},H_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I^{\prime})\neq(R_{B},H_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I^{\prime}).
Further, using Claim
11
we have that
(R_{A},H_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})\neq(R_{B},H_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(t_{\mathrm{cmp}})
. This is because after the end of a block, the values corresponding to mega-states created during that block are fixed. Now during iteration
I
, the parties copy the
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
from
\mathtt{p}_{A}(I_{A})
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}(I_{B})
, so if those values differ then
(R_{A},H_{A})(I)\neq(R_{B},H_{B})(I)
. If the difference is in the variable
\mathtt{p.T}
then both Alice and Bob append another piece of transcript and, similar to Case (a),
T_{A}^{\leq\mathtt{p}}\neq T_{B}^{\leq\mathtt{p}}
and the proof is complete.

2.

Next, suppose that
I\not\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{A}}\cap\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{B}}.

In this case, the same argument as in Case 2 of the base case applies.

This shows that (
3
) holds for all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}_{AB}}
. In particular, we have

(H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(I^{*})\neq(H_{B},R_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(I^{*}),

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary 15
.

At any time
t
and
for any
p
and
p^{\prime}
, if
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq p)}_{A}\neq\mathcal{T}^{(\leq p^{\prime})}_{B}
then
\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{B}
.

Proof.

This is a direct result of Lemma
13
. Define
\mathtt{p}_{A}^{*}=\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}(t)
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}^{*}=\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{B}(t).

Since
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq p)}\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq p^{\prime})}
, we have that,

(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},{\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{A}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}})\neq(\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.prev-seed}},{\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.T}}},\operatorname{\mathtt{\mathtt{p}}}^{*}_{B}\operatorname{\mathtt{.iter}}).

The variables in this tuple are variables of the megastates for Alice and Bob. We conclude that
\mathtt{ms}(p)\neq\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})
, which proves the statement.
∎

Corollary 16
.

If a time
t
is such that

\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t))_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t))_{B}
then

(H_{A},R_{A},{T_{A}^{\leq\mathtt{p}}},I_{H_{A}})(t)\neq(H_{B},R_{B},{T_{B}^{\leq\mathtt{p}}},I_{H_{B}})(t)\ .

Proof.

Let
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t))_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t))_{B}
. Notice that if
\mathtt{p.v}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p.v}_{B}
or
\mathtt{p.depth}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p.depth}_{B}
then
\mathcal{T}_{A}(t)\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}(t)
and using Lemma
13
we can conclude that

(H_{A},R_{A},T_{A}^{\leq\mathtt{p}},I_{H_{A}})(t)\neq(H_{B},R_{B},{T_{B}^{\leq\mathtt{p}}},I_{H_{B}})(t)\ .

Now if
\mathtt{p.v}_{A}=\mathtt{p.v}_{B}
and
\mathtt{p.depth}_{A}=\mathtt{p.depth}_{B}
, but
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
, then there exists a variable
\mathtt{var}
so that

\mathtt{var}\in\{\mathtt{p.iter},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.T},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}\},

such that
\mathtt{var}_{A}\neq\mathtt{var}_{B}
. As
\mathtt{var}
is also one of the variables of
(H,R,I)
then we can conclude that,

(H_{A},R_{A},I_{H_{A}})(t)\neq(H_{B},R_{B},I_{H_{B}})(t)\ .

this completes the proof.
∎

For the remainder of Section
5
and also in Section
6
, we will analyze Algorithm
4.2
under the assumption that
no big hash collisions ever occur
. In Section
5.4
we will show that the probability that a big hash collision occurs at some point is at most
1/\mathrm{poly}(d)
, which will be an acceptable contribution to the final probability of failure when we prove Theorem
1
in Section
5.5
.

Lemma 17
.

Fix an iteration
I
. Let
t_{0}
be the start of this iteration (Line
15
) and let
t_{1}
be the end of this iteration (Line:
68
). Further define
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{0})=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t_{0}))_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{0})=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t_{0}))_{B}
. Define
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{1})
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{1})
similarly for time
t_{1}
. We claim that, if
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{0})\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{0})
and both Alice and Bob simulate during the computation phase of iteration
I
, then
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{1})\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{1})
.

Proof.

Notice that as both parties have simulated during iteration
I
, we have that
k_{A}=k_{B}=1
. As a result, no transition will take place during this iteration. Thus, the only phase affecting the mega-states of Alice and Bob during this iteration is the computation phase.

From Corollary
16
, we have that
(H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(t_{0})\neq(H_{B},R_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(t_{0})
. During the computation phase, Alice and Bob copy the values of
H
and
R
and append this iteration’s simulation to the end of
T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A}
and
T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B}
, respectively. Hence, If any of these variables do not agree with each other at time
t_{0}
, then they will not agree at time
t_{1}
either. Further, from Claim
12
, we know that it is impossible for Alice and Bob’s megastates to only differ in the variable
\mathtt{p.iter}
, so they must differ in one of the other variables. Thus the proof is complete.

∎

We next record the following useful facts about bad spells, formalizing facts that (we hope) are intuitive.

Lemma 18

(Useful facts about bad spells)
.

Suppose that a point
b
becomes the divergent point at time
t_{0}
, and remains the divergent point until time
t_{1}
.

Then the following hold.

1.

If
t_{0}
happens within an iteration (that is, between Lines
13
and Line
68
in Algorithm
4.2
; in particular,
not
during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of a block), then one of the following will occur during that iteration:

(1)

\ell_{A}=\ell_{B}=b
and
\ell^{-}=0
before the computation phase, and afterwards
\max(\ell_{A},\ell_{B})>b
and
\ell^{-}>0
and
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A})\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B})
.

(2)

Exactly one party jumps to
b
.

2.

If
t_{0}
is not during an iteration (that is,
not
between Lines
13
through
68
), then
t_{0}
is during the Big Hash Computation phase starting at Line
69
at the end of a block.
Further, the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
exchanged during that block is corrupted during the randomness exchange in Line
70
.

3.

Fix a time
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
. For any point
p
such that
b<p\leq\min\{\ell_{A}(t),\ell_{B}(t)\}
,
\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(p)_{B}
.

4.

Let
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
. Then
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t))\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t))
.

5.

If the bad spell ends at time
t_{1}
, then
t_{1}
must occur during an iteration denoted by
I_{1}
and further, one of the following occurred at time
t_{1}
:

(1)

Both parties jumped to a point
p\leq b
.

(2)

One party jumped to
b
, while the other party was waiting at
b
.
14
14

14

For example, if Alice was “waiting at
b
,” this means that
\ell_{A}(I^{(1)})=\ell_{A}(I^{(1)}-1)=b
, and similarly for Bob.

6.

If the bad spell does not end at time
t_{1}
, then one of the following occurs:

(1)

t_{1}
is during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of a block, and the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
exchanged during that phase is corrupted.

(2)

t_{1}
is during the Transition Phase of Algorithm
4.2
, and at time
t_{1}
the divergent point changes to a point
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}<b
.

Proof.

We prove each item in turn.

1.

First, observe that the Alice and Bob’s megastates do not change during the verification phase, so
t_{0}
is either in the computation phase or the transition phase of some iteration
I^{(0)}
.

Suppose first that
t_{0}
is in the computation phase.
Since
t_{0}
is when
b
first becomes the divergent point, we claim it must be that
\ell^{-}=0
right before time
t_{0}
.
Indeed, suppose not, so there was some divergent point
b^{\prime}
prior to time
t_{0}
.
First suppose that
b^{\prime}<b
. But then
b
could not have become the divergent point, a contradiction. On the other hand, suppose that
b^{\prime}=b
. But then
b
would not have
become
the divergent point at time
t_{0}
, as it was already the divergent point. Finally, suppose that
b^{\prime}>b
. But then
\ell_{A}(t_{0}),\ell_{B}(t_{0})\geq b^{\prime}>b
, so since
b^{\prime}
is the divergent point, this means that Alice and Bob agree about
b
: that is, just before time
t_{0}

\mathtt{ms}(b)_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(b)_{B}
and
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq b)}=\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq b)}
. Since
t_{0}
is during the computation phase, neither
\mathtt{ms}(b)
nor
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq b)}
can change at time
t_{0}
for either party, so we conclude that

\mathtt{ms}(b)_{A}(t_{0})=\mathtt{ms}(b)_{B}(t_{0})\qquad\text{ and }\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq b)}(t_{0})=\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq b)}(t_{0}).

But this contradicts the assumption that
b
became the divergent point at time
t_{0}
. In any of the three cases we have a contradiction, so we conclude that
\ell^{-}=0
right before
t_{0}
.

We therefore conclude that
\ell^{-}=0
before the computation phase, i.e.,
\ell_{A}=\ell_{B}=:p
and for all
p^{\prime}\leq p
we have
\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{B}
. We now claim
p=b
. For let us consider how a bad spell can begin (which necessarily happens if
b
becomes the divergent point during the computation phase). If both parties just computed dummy rounds, then we would still have
\ell_{A}=\ell_{B}
and, as no new mega-states were created,
\ell^{-}
still equals
0
. So after the computation phase at least one party increased the length of their simulated path by
1
, implying this party’s simulated path now has length
p+1
. As a bad spell began during the computation phase, either the simulated paths or the mega-states at depth
p+1
disagree. But as these data agreed at all points up to depth
p
, it must be that
p
becomes the divergent point during the computation phase. This implies
p=b
.

Finally, we note that if after the computation phase we have
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\ell_{A})}_{A}\neq\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\ell_{B})}_{B}
then Lemma
13
implies that
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A})_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B})_{B}
. Else, if
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\ell_{A})}_{A}=\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\ell_{B})}_{B}
, since
\ell^{-}>0
after the computation phase by definition it must be that
\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(p)_{B}
.

Suppose now that
t_{0}
lies in the transition phase. Note that if neither party jumps, then the divergent point cannot change, contradicting the assumption that
b
becomes the divergent point at time
t_{0}
. Suppose now for a contradiction that both parties jump to the same point in the transition phase. If they both jump to a point
p\leq b
, then
b
could not be the divergent point at time
t_{0}
, a contradiction. If they both jump to a point
p>b
, then for
b
to be the divergent point after the transition it must have also been the divergent point prior to the transition, again a contradiction.

Thus, it cannot be that both parties jump to the same location, and that at least one party jumps. Without loss of generality, assume that Alice jumps to a point that is smaller than the point that Bob jumps to if he also jumps, or that she jumps and Bob does not. If Alice jumps to a point
p<b
, then
b
could not be the divergent point at time
t_{0}
, and similarly if
p>b
then for
b
to be the divergent point after the transition if must have also been the divergent point prior to the transition. So the only remaining possibility is that she jumps to the point
b
(and Bob does not).

2.

Notice that the only time
outside
of an iteration that a megastate is updated is during the big hash computation line
74
. As a result, if the divergent point changes at time
t_{0}
, then
t_{0}
must be during the big hash computation. Further, notice that if the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted then the agreement between megastates of Alice and Bob do not change after the big hash computation. More precisely, if two megastates
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}
were equal before time
t_{0}
, then Alice and Bob use the same inputs and same randomness to compute their big hash functions. Then
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
will be updated in the same way in the Big Hash Computation phase, and
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}
we remain the same after the Big Hash Computation phase. On the other hand, if
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
before time
t_{0}
, then according to Lemma
11
,
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
after the Big Hash Computation phase, meaning
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
at time
t_{0}
as well. Thus
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
must be corrupted if the divergent point changes at time
t_{0}
.

3.

For each time
t
we prove this statement by induction on
p
. For the base case, let
p=b+1
. Let
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(b+1)_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}=\mathtt{ms}(b+1)_{B}
. If
\mathtt{p.iter}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}
then we can conclude that
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
which proves our claim. On the other hand, if
\mathtt{p.iter}_{A}=\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}=I
where
I\in[I^{(0)},I^{(1)}]
then Alice and Bob have both simulated during iteration
I
. Now if
I=I^{(0)}
then from item 1 we know that
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
after time
t_{0}
, thus the claim is complete.

Let
p
be any point such that
b+1<p\leq\min(\ell_{A},\ell_{B})
. Define
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}=\mathtt{ms}(p)_{B}
. Then similar to the base case, if
\mathtt{p.iter}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}
then we have that
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
which proves our claim. If
\mathtt{p.iter}_{A}=\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}
, then both Alice and Bob have simulated during the computation phase. Then prior to the computation phase Alice and Bob are at depth
p-1
. By induction,
\mathtt{ms}(p-1)_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(p-1)_{B}
. Then from Lemma
17
we know that after the simulation phase
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
.

4.

We distinguish two cases. Suppose first that
\ell_{A}(t)\neq\ell_{B}(t)
. Then
\mathcal{T}_{A}(t)\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}(t)
. As we are assuming that no big hash collisions occur, Lemma
13
implies that
(H_{A},R_{A},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{A},I_{H_{A}})(t)\neq(H_{B},R_{B},T^{\leq\mathtt{p}}_{B},I_{H_{B}})(t)
, which indeed implies
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t))\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t))
.

On the other hand, if
\ell_{A}(t)=\ell_{B}(t)
, then we can apply Item 3 with
p=\ell_{A}(t)=\ell_{B}(t)
to deduce
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t))\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t))
.

5.

First, from Item 3, we observe that for a bad spell to end it must be that the parties are both at a point
p
with
p\leq b
. Indeed, at any point
p>b
we have
\mathtt{ms}(p)_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(p)_{B}
, implying that the bad spell is still ongoing.

Suppose it is not the case that both parties jumped at time
t_{1}
; without loss of generality, suppose Alice does not jump. If Alice simulated during the computation phase of
I^{(1)}
, then she must have been at point
p-1
prior to this round; since
p-1<p\leq b
, this contradicts the assumption that
b
is the divergent point. Furthermore, if she waited in this iteration (i.e., she did dummy computations in the computation phase) and
p<b
, then again we contradict the fact that
b
was the divergent point at the start of
I^{(1)}
. We conclude that she must have been waiting at point
b
.

As the bad spell was still ongoing at the start of iteration
I^{(1)}
, Bob must have not been at point
b
, and as
b
is the divergent point he must have been at a point
p>b
. Since both Alice and Bob must be at the same point at time
t_{1}
, it follows that Bob must have jumped to the point
b
at time
t_{1}
.

6.

If the bad spell does not end at time
t_{1}
, but
b
is no longer the divergent point after time
t_{1}
, then it must be that the divergent point changes at time
t_{1}
; let
b^{\prime}\neq b
be the new divergent point. If
b^{\prime}>b
, then we know from Item 3 that
\mathtt{ms}(b^{\prime})_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(b^{\prime})_{B}
, implying that
b^{\prime}
cannot be a divergent point. Hence, it must be that
b^{\prime}<b
. Note that as
b
was a divergent point, by definition it must be that

\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq b^{\prime})}=\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq b^{\prime})}\text{ and }\forall p^{\prime}\leq b^{\prime},~\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(p^{\prime})_{B}\ .

First assume that
t_{1}
is during an iteration, and denote this iteration by
I_{1}
. Then
as
b^{\prime}
is now the divergent point, we either have that (a) at least one party’s simulated path is of length most
b^{\prime}
, or that(b) both parties have simulated paths of length at least
b^{\prime\prime}>b^{\prime}
, but the simulated paths disagree at depths
b^{\prime}+1,\dots,b^{\prime\prime}
. As
b
was the divergent point before time
t_{1}
, it must be that the simulated paths had agreed at depths
b^{\prime}+1,\dots,b
, showing that case (b) case is in fact impossible, and (a) holds: at least one party’s simulated path has length at most
b^{\prime}
. Suppose without loss of generality that this party was Alice; then Alice’s simulated path had length
exactly

b^{\prime}
at time
t_{1}
(or else the divergent point would be higher). Since
\ell_{A}\geq b>b^{\prime}
before time
t_{1}
, it must be that she jumped back to the point
b^{\prime}
at time
t_{1}
. We also note that both Alice and Bob could not have both jumped back to
b^{\prime}
, as otherwise—appealing to the proof of the previous part—the bad spell would have ended at time
t_{1}
. Thus, one party jumped back to
b^{\prime}
, which must occur during the transition phase of Algorithm
4.2
. It follows that
t_{1}
is during the transition phase of Algorithm
4.2
and that the new divergent point is
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}<b
, as claimed.

Next, assume that
t_{1}
is not during an iteration. By Item 2,
t_{1}
must be during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of the block. As no megastates are created during the Big Hash Computation phase, there must be two megastates
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}
such that
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{p}_{B}
prior to Big Hash Computation phase and
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
after the Big Hash Computation phase. If
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted, then with a similar argument to Item 2, we have that the agreement/disagreement status between megastates of Alice and Bob do not change during the big hash computation. Hence,
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
must be corrupted.

∎

We begin by giving an upper bound for the number of iterations that either have corrupted randomness or corrupted communication. In particular, we show that the they are both on the order of
\varepsilon d
, which is the adversary’s corruption budget.

Lemma 19
.

Define
Q
as the total number of iterations with either corrupted communication or corrupted randomness. Then,
Q=O(\varepsilon d)
.

Proof.

We count the number of iterations suffering from each type of corruption separately.
For an iteration to have a corrupted randomness either the randomness shared at the start of the block is corrupted or the randomness shared at the start of the iteration is corrupted. The total number of iterations having a corrupted randomness on iteration level is at most
2\varepsilon d
as this is the maximum number of iterations in which the adversary can introduce corruptions. To count the number of iterations with corrupted randomness shared at block level, notice that according to Theorem
6
the randomness exchange is protected by an ECC with minimum distance
4I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
. Then the adversary must invest in at least
2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
corruptions to corrupt this randomness. As a result there are at most
\frac{2\varepsilon d}{2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}
blocks having corrupted randomness which means that there are at most
\frac{\varepsilon d}{I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=\varepsilon d
iterations having corrupted randomness.
Finally, similar to randomness exchanged at iteration level, there are at most
2\varepsilon d
iterations having corrupted communication. Adding all cases together, there are at most
O(\varepsilon d)
iterations having either corrupted randomness or corrupted communication, i.e.,
Q=O(\varepsilon d)
as claimed.
∎

Finally, we prove a few useful lemma about meeting points.

Lemma 20

(jumps go above stable points)
.

Let
p
be a
w
-stable point, and suppose that
p
is removed from Alice’s meeting point set
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t
.
Suppose that
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
is the next time after
t
that Alice jumps to a point
q<p+2^{w}
. Then
q\leq p
.

Proof.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
q>p
.
Suppose that
q
is
u
-stable, for some
u<w
; note that we must indeed have
u<w
, because by assumption
q\in(p,p+2^{w})
and thus lies strictly between two consecutive multiples of
2^{w}
.

By Lemma
8
, the point
p
will be removed from Alice’s memory when she first reaches a depth of
c_{p}=p+2^{w+1}
.

Now we claim that
c_{p}<q+2^{u+1}
. To see this, we first observe that Lemma
2
implies that
q\in M_{c_{p}}
if and only if
c_{p}\in[q,q+2^{u+1}-1],
so as long as
q\in M_{c_{p}}
, we will have
c_{p}<q+2^{u+1}
.

Next we show that
q\in M_{c_{p}}
.
As
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}(t_{\mathrm{jump}})
(since Alice jumped to
q
at that time), if
q\not\in M_{c_{p}}=M_{\ell_{A}(t)}
, then Alice had “forgotten”
q
by time
t
, so we must have
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})=q
for some
t^{\prime}\in(t,t_{\mathrm{jump}})
, so that
q
can have been added back before Alice jumped there. But the only way to add
q
back would have involved jumping to a point
q^{\prime}\leq q
at some time
t^{\prime\prime}\in(t,t^{\prime}]\subseteq(t,t_{\mathrm{jump}})
, contradicting our assumption that
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
was the next time after
t
that Alice jumped to a point above
p+2^{w}
.

Therefore,
q\in M_{c_{p}}
, and we conclude by the reasoning above that
c_{p}<q+2^{u+1}
. Since
u<w
by the above, we see that
c_{p}<q+2^{w}.

Thus, recalling the definition of
c_{p}
, we have

p+2^{w+1}\leq c_{p}<q+2^{w},

implying that
q>p+2^{w}.
However, this is false, since we have assumed that
q<p+2^{w}
.

∎

Suppose that Alice and Bob are voting on a shared meeting point, so the parameters
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
are increasing. When
k_{A}=k_{B}\in\{2^{j},\ldots,2^{j+1}-1\}
, they are “voting” for a shared scale-
j
transition candidate, if it exists.
Intuitively, if Alice and Bob “skip” such a transition candidate (that is, they do not jump when
k_{A},k_{B}
are in this window), then the adversary had to introduce on the order of
2^{j}
corruptions in order to make them do that. We formalize this intuition in the following lemma.

Lemma 21

(The adversary must pay to “skip” a meeting point)
.

Fix a time
t^{\dagger}
in iteration
I^{\dagger}=\mathcal{I}(t^{\dagger})
, and suppose that
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})=:k
.
Suppose that there is some
j<\lfloor\log k\rfloor
and some point
q
so that

q\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(j,\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger}))\cap\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(j,\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})),

(4)

and that
q
is an available jumpable point for Alice and Bob at time
t^{\dagger}
.
Then there are at least
(0.6)2^{j}
iterations
I
in the window
W=\{I^{\dagger}-k+1,I^{\dagger}-k+2,\ldots,I^{\dagger}\}
such that during iteration
I
,
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
increases by at least one. In particular
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}(t^{\dagger})\geq(0.6)2^{j}
.

Proof.

First, notice that, because
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})
,
k_{A}(I)=k_{B}(I)
for each
I\in W
; indeed, in each iteration,
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
either increase by one or get reset to zero, so the only way
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})=k
is if each have increased by one in each iteration for the past
k
iterations. Further, this logic implies that both Alice and Bob ran dummy rounds (that is, Line
53
in Algorithm
4.2
was executed) during the window
W
, because otherwise
k_{A}
or
k_{B}
would have been reset to zero. In particular none of the quantities
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A},\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B},\ell_{A},
or
\ell_{B}
change during the window
W
. Further,
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}
and
\mathrm{BVC}_{B}
do not decrease during the window
W
, as by Definition
10
,
\mathrm{BVC}
only decreases when either Alice or Bob reset their status, meaning that either
k_{A}
or
k_{B}
gets reset to zero, which by the above does not happen during
W
.

This discussion, along with (
4
), implies that

\forall I\in W\text{ s.t. }j_{A}(I)=j_{B}(I)=j,\qquad q\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}_{A}(I)\cap\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}_{B}(I).

(5)

That is, for all iterations
I
in the last
k
iterations before
I^{\dagger}
such that
j_{A}=j_{B}=j
during iteration
I
,
q
is a transition candidate for both Alice and Bob.

Now consider the iterations
I^{\ast}
so that
k_{A}(I^{*}),k_{B}(I^{*})\in[2^{j},2^{j+1}-1]
(observing that all of these iterations lie in
W
, due to our assumption that
j<\lfloor\log k\rfloor
).

Note that for these iterations, we have
j_{A}(I^{\ast})=j_{B}(I^{\ast})=j
. We claim that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
increases by at least one for at least
(0.6)2^{j}
of these iterations. This will establish the lemma,
using the fact that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
cannot decrease in the window
W
. To establish the claim, fix such an
I^{\ast}
and let
i\in\{1,2,3\}
be such that
q=\mathrm{MPi}_{A}
during
I^{*}
. Then since, by (
5
),
q\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}_{B}(I^{*})
, the only way that
v^{(i)}_{A}
does not increment in line
40
is if
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}
increments (see the Definition
10
of
\mathrm{BVC}
, and recall that since
q
is an available jumpable point, this means that not only is
q
available for both Alice and Bob, but also the mega-state it corresponds to is the same for both of them).
If
v^{(i)}_{A}>(0.4)2^{j}
at iteration
2^{j+1}-1
, Alice would have jumped to
q
in line
63
and she did not, so we conclude
v^{(i)}_{A}
did not increment, and hence
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}
did, for at least
(0.6)2^{j}
iterations. Using the definition that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}=\mathrm{BVC}_{A}+\mathrm{BVC}_{B}
, this proves the claim and the lemma.
∎

5.2
Sneaky Attack and Main Technical Lemma

In this section, we formally define a sneaky attack, and state and prove several useful lemmas about sneaky attacks.
As discussed in Section
2
, a sneaky attack is the only exception to the intuition that whenever
k_{A}=k_{B}
gets large (relative to
L^{-}
), then the adversary has to “pay” by increasing
\mathrm{BVC}
.

We also state our main technical lemma, Lemma
31
, which roughly says that
almost
all of the time that
k_{A}=k_{B}
gets large (relative to
L^{-}
), the adversary had to pay by increasing
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
.

We begin with an auxiliary definition, and then formally define a sneaky attack.

Definition 11

(The constant
c^{*}
)
.

Fix a constant
c^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that
c^{*}\geq 3
. This constant will appear in the definition of a sneaky attack below, and we will choose
c^{*}
to be sufficiently large later on in the analysis.

Definition 12

(Sneaky attack)
.

Fix a time
t^{\dagger}
and suppose that
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})=:k
.
Suppose that

\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})>0
.
Let
j_{p}
be the next jumpable scale at time
t^{\dagger}
, and
suppose that
p
is a next available jumpable point .
Let
\ell_{A}=\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})
and
\ell_{B}=\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})
.
Let
w
be such that
p
is a scale-
w
MP for Alice.

Let
\hat{p}=p+2^{w}
,
q=p+2^{w-1}
, and
c_{q}=q+2^{w}
.
Suppose that
b
is the divergent point at time
t^{\dagger}
.
We say that a
sneaky attack for Alice heading towards
p
is in progress at time
t^{\dagger}

if
k\leq 2^{w+1}
, and
if
there are times
t_{\hat{p}}<t_{c_{q}}<t_{\hat{q}}<t_{b}<t^{\dagger}
so that all of the following hold:

•

\ell_{A}(t_{c_{q}})=c_{q}
. Further,
t_{c_{q}}
is the last time that this happens before
t^{\dagger}
. That is,

t_{c_{q}}=\max\{t\leq t^{\dagger}\,:\,\ell_{A}(t)=c_{q}\}.

•

t_{\hat{p}}=\max\{t<t_{c_{q}}\,:\,\ell_{A}(t)=\hat{p}\}
is the last time that Alice passes
\hat{p}
before
t_{c_{q}}
.

•

At time
t_{\hat{q}}
, at least one of Alice and Bob jumps to a meeting point
\hat{q}\geq\hat{p}

to end a bad spell; in particular,
\ell^{-}(t_{\hat{q}})=0
,
\ell_{A}(t_{\hat{q}})=\ell_{B}(t_{\hat{q}})=\hat{q}
.
15
15

15

As per Lemma
18
, either
both
Alice and Bob jumped to
\hat{q}
simultaneously; or only one of them did, but the other was already there and simulated dummy rounds.
Moreover,
t_{\hat{q}}
is the first time that
\ell^{-}
is reset to zero after time
t_{c_{q}}
, and

\hat{q}=\hat{p}
.
16
16

16

The reason we give
\hat{q}
a different name (even though it is equal to
\hat{p}
) is because the time
t_{\hat{p}}
is already defined, so we use
t_{\hat{q}}
to denote the time of the jump, and use the letter
\hat{q}
for the point itself to not cause confusion. We will use the fact that
\hat{q}\geq\hat{p}
often, but the fact that
\hat{q}=\hat{p}
only occasionally.

•

Bob jumps to
b
at time
t_{b}
, and further
b\geq\hat{p}-2^{w-c^{\ast}}
. The divergent point becomes
b
at time
t_{b}
, and
b
remains the divergent point for all times in
[t_{b},t^{\dagger}]
.

•

For all
t^{\prime}\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{b})
,
\ell_{B}(t^{\prime})<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
; and
\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})<\hat{p}+2^{w-2}
.

•

At time
t^{\dagger}
,
q
is not in Alice’s memory. That is,
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t^{\dagger}
.

A
sneaky attack for Bob
is defined analogously (switching the roles of Alice and Bob in the above); note that in this case
p
would be a scale-
w
MP for Bob, rather than for Alice.

We refer the reader to Figure
2
for a picture of a sneaky attack.

Observation 22
.

We make the following observations about the definition of a sneaky attack.

(a)

Observe that
t_{c_{q}}
is the time at which Alice will “forget”
q
. Indeed,

q
is
(w-1)
-stable, as it lies between two multiples of
2^{w}
, and Lemma
8
implies that Alice forgets it at depth
q+2^{w}=c_{q}
.

(b)

Observe that for any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
, we have
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}
. Indeed, by the definition of
t_{\hat{p}}
, this holds for any
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{c_{q}}]
; otherwise since
c_{q}>\hat{p}
, Alice would have had to cross
\hat{p}
at some time in
[t,t_{c_{q}}]
, contradicting the choice of
t_{\hat{p}}
as the last time before
t_{c_{q}}
that this occured.

Now suppose that for some
t\in[t_{c_{q}},t^{\dagger}]
, we have
\ell_{A}(t)<\hat{p}
; say
t
is the first such time. By Lemma
2
, none of the points between
q
and
\hat{p}-1
are available for Alice after she has passed
c_{q}
, as
c_{q}\geq q+2^{w}
.
Since Alice only jumps to available meeting points,
this means that the first time after
t_{c_{q}}
that Alice jumps above
\hat{p}
, she in fact must jump above
q
. This implies that
\ell_{A}(t)<q
.
However, at time
t^{\dagger}>t
,
p
is an order-
w
MP for Alice, which means that
\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})>p+2^{w}=\hat{p}
. Thus, there was some time
t^{*}\in(t,t^{\dagger})
so that
\ell_{A}(t^{*})=q
. But then
q
will be re-added to
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t^{*}
. Since
t_{c_{q}}<t^{*}
was the last time before
t^{\dagger}
that Alice reached
c_{q}
, part (a) of the Observation implies that
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t^{\dagger}
as well. But this contradicts the last part of Definition
12
.

We next define a few auxiliary notions surrounding sneaky attacks. The first has to do with the “end” of a sneaky attack, when Alice and Bob are voting about whether to jump to the point
p
.

Definition 13

(Voting window; sneaky jump)
.

If a sneaky attack is in progress at time
t^{\dagger}
, then by definition
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})=k
, which implies that for iterations in the window
W=\{I^{\dagger}-k+1,\ldots,I^{\dagger}\}
, Alice and Bob have been simulating dummy rounds and the parameters
\ell_{A},\ell_{B}
have not changed.
17
17

17

Indeed, these dummy rounds are simulated in Line
53
of Algorithm
4.2
; notice that the dummy rounds are simulated only if
k>1
. Further, if Line
53
is not executed, then
k
gets reset to
0
and hence will be
1
at the beginning of the next iteration, so running dummy rounds is the only way that
k
can increment over several iterations.
We call this window
W
the
voting window for the sneaky attack.

Suppose that, at some time
t_{\mathrm{jump}}\geq t^{\dagger}
, Alice and Bob have continued voting (that is, neither
k_{A}
nor
k_{B}
have been reset to
0
between
t^{\dagger}
and
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
), and then they successfully jump to
p
to resolve the bad spell at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
. Then we say that the sneaky attack is
completed
, and we call the jump at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
a
sneaky jump
.

We refer to the time interval
[t_{\hat{p}},t_{\mathrm{jump}}]
as the
time window for the sneaky attack
.

The next definition has to do with the part of the sneaky attack where Alice is getting “driven down” (Step 2 in Figure
2
).

Definition 14
.

Suppose that a sneaky attack towards
p
is in progress at time
t^{\dagger}
(say, for Alice), and let
w
be the scale of
p
for Alice. Let
t_{\hat{p}},t_{c_{q}}
be as in Definition
12
. Define the
diving window for the sneaky attack
to be the set
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
of iterations
I
between time
t_{\hat{p}}
and
t_{c_{q}}
where:

•

There is some
\ell\in[\hat{p},c_{q}]
so that Alice simulates
\Pi
at depth
\ell
for the last time in
[t_{\hat{p}},t_{c_{q}}]
; and

•

\ell^{-}>0
.

That is, intuitively we would like to define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
to be the set of iterations where Alice is getting “driven down” below Bob. However, Alice may jump during this phase, and for technical reasons we want her to be at a different point for every iteration in the diving window. Thus, we just count the iterations where she is at a particular point for the
last
time during this window.

Remark 11
.

If
S
is a sneaky attack with a jump of scale
w
, then
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|=2^{w-1}
. The reason for this is that the diving window contains an iteration for each depth between
[\hat{p},c_{q}]
. As a result we have that,
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|=c_{q}-\hat{p}=(q+2^{w})-(p+2^{w})=q-p=2^{w-1}
.

Lemma 23
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
is a diving window, and that

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{T}\}

for some
I_{1}<I_{2}<\cdots<I_{T}
. Then
for any
i=1,\ldots,T-1
,
\ell_{A}(I_{i})<\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
, and there is no
I\in(I_{i},I_{i+1})
so that
\ell_{A}(I)\leq\ell_{A}(I_{i})
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})<\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
by induction on
i
, starting with
i=T-1
. Suppose that
i=T-1
. Then
\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})=\ell_{A}(I_{T})=c_{q}
, since by definition
I_{T}
is the iteration in which the diving window ends, namely the iteration where Alice reaches
c_{q}
. This implies that
\ell_{A}(I_{T-1})<\ell_{A}(I_{T})
, since
\ell_{A}(I_{j})\leq c_{q}
for all
I_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
, and
\ell_{A}(I_{j})
is distinct for each
I_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
.
Now fix some
i^{*}<T-1
and assume that for all
i\geq i^{*}
,
\ell_{A}(I_{i})<\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
. Consider
i=i^{*}-1
. We claim that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})<\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})=\ell_{A}(I_{i^{*}})
. Indeed, suppose that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})>\ell_{A}(I_{i^{*}})
. But then there is some
j\geq i^{*}
so that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})\in[\ell_{A}(I_{j}),\ell_{A}(I_{j+1})]
, where we have used the inductive hypothesis for
j
. In particular there is some
I\in[I_{j},I_{j+1}]
so that
\ell_{A}(I)=\ell_{A}(I_{i})
. But this contradicts the choice of
I_{i}
as the
last
iteration when Alice was at
\ell_{A}(I_{i})
.
Thus (using the fact that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})\neq\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
, as all the values
\ell_{A}(I_{i})
are distinct), we conclude that
\ell_{A}(I_{i})<\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
.
This establishes the inductive hypothesis and the claim.

Now we prove the second statement. Fix
i
and
let
I\in(I_{i},I_{i+1})
. If
\ell_{A}(I)\leq\ell_{A}(I_{i})
, then there must be some
I^{\prime}\in[I,I_{i+1}]\subseteq(I_{i},I_{i+1}]
so that
\ell_{A}(I^{\prime})=\ell_{A}(I_{i})
, since Alice must cross
\ell_{A}(I_{i})
again on her way to
\ell_{A}(I_{i+1})
. But this contradicts the choice of
I_{i}
as the last iteration in which Alice reaches
\ell_{A}(I_{i})
.
∎

Lemma 24

(There are many corruptions or small hash collisions during a sneaky attack)
.

Suppose there are no big hash collisions throughout Algorithm
4.2
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be a sneaky attack in progress for Alice towards a point
p
.
Then in at least
(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|
iterations in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
, at least one of the following occurred:

•

There was a dangerous small hash collision.

•

The adversary inserted a corruption.

•

There was corrupted randomness for that iteration (Definition
9
).

Above,
c^{*}
is as in Definition
11
.

Proof.

Notice for any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{c_{q}})
, we have from Definition
12
that
\ell_{B}(t)<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
. (Indeed, Definition
12
says that this holds until
t_{b}>t_{c_{q}}
.)

Thus for any iteration
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
such that
\ell_{A}(I)\geq\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
we have
\ell_{A}(I)\neq\ell_{B}(I)
.

Next, let
I^{\ast}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
be the iteration where
\ell_{A}(I^{\ast})=\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}+1
. Note that such an iteration must exist because
c_{q}\geq\hat{p}+2^{w-1}>\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
, and there is an iteration in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
for each point between
\hat{p}
and
c_{q}
.
Lemma
23
implies that for any iteration
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
after (and including)
I^{\ast}
and before time
t_{c_{q}}
, we have
\ell_{A}(I)\geq\ell_{A}(I^{*})>\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
, and in particular
\ell_{A}(I)\neq\ell_{B}(I)
for all such
I
.

Fix an iteration
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
after
I^{*}
and before time
t_{c_{q}}
, By Lemma
13
, the tuple

(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter})

that Alice and Bob have computed at the beginning of iteration
I
will not match. Also note that, since that
\ell_{A}(I)\neq\ell_{B}(I)
, iteration
I
is a dangerous iteration.

In order for the simulation for Alice to proceed during the computation phase of iteration
I
, the check of
(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter})
on Line
43
must pass, which means that the adversary must have corrupted Bob’s transmitted small hash value
H_{\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.iter}}
; there was corrupted randomness in iteration
I
; or there was a dangerous small collision.

Finally we count the number of such iterations
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
after
I^{\ast}
. There are at most
2^{w-c^{*}}
iterations in the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
that can occur before
I^{\ast}
(using Lemma
23
to assert that the depths of the iterations in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}
are increasing). This leaves at least

|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|-2^{w-c^{*}}

iterations remaining. Since by Remark
11
we have
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|=2^{w-1}
, then there are at least
(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|
iterations in the diving window containing either corruption or dangerous small hash collision. This concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma 25

(Sneaky attacks are not too tall)
.

Let
I
be an iteration during the voting window of a sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
for Alice towards a point
p
.
Let
H_{\mathcal{S}}
be the number of dangerous small hash collisions that occur during the diving window of
\mathcal{S}
, and similarly
Q_{\mathcal{S}}
be the number of iterations having corrupted randomness or corrupted communication during the diving window of
\mathcal{S}
.
Then
k_{A}(I)=k_{B}(I)=k
where
k\leq\alpha(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}})
for some absolute constant
\alpha
.

Proof.

To prove the statement, we first show that the maximum number of iterations in a diving window has an upper bound of
c^{\prime}(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}})
. Then, by relating the size of the diving window to the size of the voting window we can show the desired bound on
k
.
From Lemma
24
, we know that
in at least
(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|
iterations in the diving window, there is either a dangerous small hash collision, corrupted communication or corrupted randomness.
This implies that

(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|\leq Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}}.

Then setting
c^{\prime}=\frac{1}{(1-2^{1-c^{*}})}
we immediately get that
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|\leq c^{\prime}(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{S})
.

Let
w
be the scale of the meeting point
p
for Alice, so Remark
11
implies that
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|=2^{w-1}
.
Thus, the above implies that

2^{w-1}\leq c^{\prime}(q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}}).

Further, we have
k\leq 2^{w+1}
from the definition of a sneaky attack. Thus, we have

k\leq 16c^{\prime}(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}}).

Setting
\alpha=16c^{\prime}
proves the statement.
∎

Lemma 26

(Diving windows and voting windows are disjoint)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
be two distinct sneaky attacks that resolve with two sneaky jumps.
Then the voting windows and the diving windows for
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are all pairwise disjoint.

Proof.

Each sneaky attack has a series of points and times as per Definition
12
. We subscript these with
i\in\{1,2\}
to indicate which sneaky attack we are talking about. That is, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is towards the meeting point
p_{i}
, which is at scale
w_{i}
for whichever party the attack is for, and involves points
\hat{p}_{i},\hat{q}_{i},q_{i},
and
c_{q_{i}}
; and times
t_{\hat{p}_{i}}<t_{c_{q_{i}}}<t_{\hat{q}_{i}}<t_{b_{i}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{i}}
.

Claim 27
.

The voting windows (c.f. Definition
13
) of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are disjoint.

Proof.

We have two possible scenarios. Either
t_{\mathrm{jump_{1}}}\neq t_{\mathrm{jump_{2}}}
or
t_{\mathrm{jump_{1}}}=t_{\mathrm{jump_{2}}}=:t_{\mathrm{jump}}
.

For the first scenario, without loss of generality assume that
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}
.
At time
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
, the counters
k_{A},k_{B}
are reset to
0
as part of the sneaky jump. This implies that the voting window for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
begins after time
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
, since during the voting window the counters
k_{A},k_{B}
are increasing.
Hence the voting windows are disjoint.

Now we consider the second scenario, where
t_{\mathrm{jump_{1}}}=t_{\mathrm{jump_{2}}}=t_{\mathrm{jump}}
. This scenario is more involved, and there are two cases: either both sneaky attacks are for the same party, or they are for different parties.

1.

Both sneaky attacks are for the same party.
Without loss of generality assume both
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are for Alice. As both
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
complete at the same time, they must complete at the same point
p_{1}=p_{2}=:p
; and since they are both for Alice, the scale of
p
for Alice in both attacks is the same. Thus,
w_{1}=w_{2}=:w
. Now knowing the jump destination
p
and the scale of the jump
w
uniquely defines the sneaky attack. Indeed, given
p
and
w
, the points
\hat{p},q,\hat{q}
and
c_{q}
are determined;
b
is determined since it is the divergent point at the time of the jump.
Further, the times involved in the attack are also determined: We set
t^{\dagger}=t_{\mathrm{jump}}
; the time
t_{b}
is determined as it is the time that
b
became the divergent point; the time
t_{c_{q}}
is determined as it is the last time that Alice reached
c_{q}
before
t^{\dagger}
; the time
t_{\hat{p}}
is determined as the last time before
t_{c_{q}}
that Alice passed
\hat{p}
; and the time
t_{\hat{q}}
is the first time after
t_{c_{q}}
that
\ell^{-}=0
.

We conclude that
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{2}
, a contradiction. Thus this case cannot occur.

2.

The two sneaky attacks are for different parties.
As before, since both attacks resolve with the same jump, we have
p_{1}=p_{2}=:p.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is for Alice, so
p
is a scale
w_{1}
meeting point for Alice. Similarly,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is for Bob, and
p
is a scale
w_{2}
meeting point for Bob. Furthermore, suppose without loss of generality that
w_{1}\geq w_{2}
. Notice that when
w_{1}\geq w_{2}
, then
\hat{p}_{1}\geq\hat{p}_{2}
.
Indeed, this is true because

\hat{p}_{1}=p+2^{w_{1}}\geq p+2^{w_{2}}=\hat{p_{2}}.

During the attack
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, Alice jumps to a point
b_{2}
at time
t_{b_{2}}
, where
b_{2}<\hat{p}_{2}\leq\hat{p}_{1}
.
By Observation
(b)
(b), we have
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}_{1}
for all times
t\in[t_{\hat{p}_{1}},t_{\mathrm{jump}}]
. Thus,
t_{b_{2}}
cannot lie in this interval, so
t_{b_{2}}<t_{\hat{p}_{1}}
. However, from the definition of a sneaky attack for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, this means that
b_{2}
is the divergent point for the entire interval
[t_{b_{2}},t_{\mathrm{jump}})
. This is a contradiction because from the definition of a sneaky attack for
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, there is a point
t_{\hat{q}_{1}}\in(t_{\hat{p}_{1}},t_{\mathrm{jump}})\subseteq[t_{b_{2}},t_{\mathrm{jump}})
where a bad spell ends, and there is
no
divergent point.

This proves the claim.
∎

Claim 28
.

The voting window
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is disjoint from the diving window of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
; and vice versa.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we prove that the voting window of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is disjoint from the diving window of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
.
During iterations included in the voting window of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
,
Alice and Bob are both stationary in the robust protocol
\Pi^{\prime}
and do not simulate the original protocol
\Pi
. This is in contrast to iterations in the diving window of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, which correspond to iterations in which at least one of the parties is simulating the original protocol
\Pi
. Hence voting windows and diving windows cannot overlap, proving the claim.
∎

Claim 29
.

The diving windows of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are disjoint from each other.

Proof.

If the time windows of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are disjoint from each other (that is, if
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}\leq t_{\hat{p}_{2}}
or
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}\leq t_{\hat{p}_{1}}
), then the diving windows are also disjoint. Thus, suppose that
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
overlap in time. Either the time window of one attack is contained in the other, or one attack begins during the other attack and continues for a longer duration. Each case is treated separately.

Case 1:
