The time window of one attack starts during the other attack and continues after the other is completed.

Without loss of generality assume that
t_{\hat{p}_{1}}\leq t_{\hat{p}_{2}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}
and that
\mathcal{S}_{1}
was directed towards Alice.

Recall from Observation
(b)
that for any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}_{1}},t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}]
,
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}_{1}
, and
from Definition
12
we have that for any such
t
,
\ell_{B}(t)\geq\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
.
In particular,
\ell_{A}(t_{\hat{p}_{2}}),\ell_{B}(t_{\hat{p}_{2}})\geq\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
.
Since at least one of Alice and Bob is at
\hat{p}_{2}
at time
t_{\hat{p}_{2}}
(depending on the target of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
), we conclude that

\hat{p}_{2}\geq\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}.

Notice that at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
, both Alice and Bob simultaneously jump to point
p_{1}
, where
p_{1}<\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}\leq\hat{p}_{2}
.
We claim that this leads to a contradiction. Indeed, for whichever party
\mathcal{S}_{2}
was against, that party must remain
below

\hat{p}_{2}
during the interval
[t_{\hat{p}_{2}},t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}]
, by Observation
(b)
(b). But
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
lies in that interval, and we have just seen that both parties jump
above

\hat{p}_{2}
at that time.
Thus this case cannot occur.

Case 2:

The time window of one attack is completely contained in the other attack.
Again without loss of generality assume that
t_{\hat{p}_{1}}\leq t_{\hat{p}_{2}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
, so the time window for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is contained in that of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Furthermore assume without loss of generality that the attack
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is for Alice. Then for all
t\in[t_{\hat{p}_{1}},t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}]
, we have that
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}_{1}
and
\ell_{B}(t)\geq\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
.
(Indeed, the first is from Observation
(b)
(b); the second follows because if Bob went to a point less than
\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
during that interval, he would have gone higher than
b
, which by Definition
12
satisfies
b\geq\hat{p}_{1}-2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
. But then the divergent point at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
would have been less than
b
, since Alice could not have resolved it, a contradiction.)

Note that if
\hat{p}_{2}\leq\hat{p}_{1}
then at the time
t_{\mathrm{jump}_{2}}<t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}}
, Alice and Bob simultaneously jump to a common point strictly above
\hat{p}_{1}
, and in particular
\ell_{A}(t_{\mathrm{jump}_{1}})<\hat{p}_{1}
, contradicting the above. Therefore
\hat{p}_{2}>\hat{p}_{1}
.

Now we have two subcases, depending on whether
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}
occurs before or after
t_{c_{q_{1}}}
.

Subcase 1:

t_{\hat{q}_{2}}<t_{c_{q_{1}}}
,
that is, Alice and Bob jump to
\hat{q}_{2}
before Alice reaches
c_{q_{1}}
.

In this scenario we will show that the diving windows are disjoint. Notice that according to Definition
12
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\hat{q}_{i}=\hat{p}_{i}
. Now take any depth
\ell\in[\hat{p}_{2},c_{q_{2}}]
, let
I_{1}
be the iteration in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}_{1}}
corresponding to this depth and similarly define
I_{2}
for
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}_{2}}
. Then
I_{1}>I_{2}
because at time
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}
both Alice and Bob jump to the point
\hat{p}_{2}
and as a result in order for Alice to reach the point
c_{q_{1}}\geq\ell
she needs to pass the depth
\ell
at least one more time. Thus
I_{2}
is not the last time Alice has visited
\ell
before
c_{q_{1}}
in
\mathcal{S}_{1}
. This holds for all
\ell
in the defined interval thus the diving windows are disjoint. (We note that they are disjoint as
sets
, although they may be interleaved.)

Subcase 2:

t_{\hat{q}_{2}}>t_{c_{q_{1}}}
,
that is, Alice and Bob jump to
\hat{q}_{2}
after Alice reaches the point
c_{q_{1}}.
We will show that this scenario is not possible.
First, we claim that

\hat{q}_{2}=\hat{p}_{2}\leq\hat{p}_{1}+2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}.

Indeed, this is because Bob must stay above
\hat{p}_{1}+2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
until
t_{b_{1}}
. Further, we claim that
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}<t_{b_{1}}
, which would imply that
\hat{q}_{2}\leq\hat{p}_{1}+2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
, as desired. To see why
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}<t_{b_{1}}
, for the sake of contradiction, assume otherwise. Then, according to Definition
12
, for the attack
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, the bad spell starting at
t_{b_{1}}
ends at
{t_{\mathrm{jump}}}_{1}
with the jump to
p_{1}
. As a result, we have
\ell^{-}>0
throughout the window
[t_{b_{1}},{t_{\mathrm{jump}}}_{1})
. This means that if
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}\in[t_{b_{1}},{t_{\mathrm{jump}}}_{1})
, then
\ell^{-}(t_{\hat{q}_{2}})>0
and thus Alice and Bob cannot end a bad spell for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
at time
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}
, contradicting the definition of a sneaky attack for
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. This implies that
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}<t_{b_{1}}
and hence
\hat{q}_{2}\leq\hat{p}_{q}+2^{w_{1}-c^{*}}
.

However, at time
t_{c_{q_{1}}}
, Alice reaches
c_{q_{1}}
and has forgotten all points in the range
(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{1}+2^{w_{1}-2})
, which includes
\hat{q}_{2}
, as
c^{*}>2
. But this is a contradiction, as Alice would not be able to jump to
\hat{q}_{2}
at time
t_{\hat{q}_{2}}
.

∎

This completes the proof of the claim, which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 30

(The total rewind due to sneaky attacks is bounded)
.

Let
S:=\{\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2},...,\mathcal{S}_{m}\}
be defined as the set of all sneaky attacks that were completed during the execution of Algorithm
4.2
.
Define
\ell^{\ast}
to be the total rewind length caused by all the sneaky attacks, i.e.,
\ell^{\ast}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Delta\ell^{+}(t_{\mathrm{jump_{i}}})
,
where
\Delta\ell^{+}(t_{\mathrm{jump_{i}}})
denotes the change in the value of
\ell^{+}
caused by the jump occurring at time
t_{\mathrm{jump_{i}}}
. Then
\ell^{\ast}\leq c(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})
for some constant
c
, where
Q
is the total number of iterations having corrupted communication or randomness during Algorithm
4.2
, and
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
is the total number of dangerous small hash collisions during dangerous iterations throughout Algorithm
4.2
.

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
24
that, for every sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}_{i}
,
there are at least
(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|
iterations where either a small hash collision, a corruption, or an iteration with corrupted randomness occurred.
In particular, this implies that

(1-2^{1-c^{*}})|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|\leq Q_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}+H_{\mathcal{S}_{i}},

where
Q_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
is the number of iterations having corrupted randomness or communication during
\mathcal{S}_{i}
, and
H_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
is the number of dangerous small hash collisions during
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

By Claim
29
, we know that the diving windows of sneaky attacks are disjoint, as a result each small hash collision corresponds to diving window of at most one sneaky attack. Then,
\sum_{i=0}^{k}H_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\leq\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
.

Similarly, each corruption also corresponds to at most a single sneaky attack, which implies that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}Q_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\leq Q
. As a result,

\sum_{i=0}^{k}|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive},i}|\leq c^{\prime}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k}Q_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}+\sum_{i=0}^{k}H_{S_{i}}\right)\leq c^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}),

where
c^{\prime}=1/(1-2^{1-c^{*}})
is a constant, and where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive},i}
is the diving window for
\mathcal{S}_{i}
.

Finally, let
\mathcal{S}
be a sneaky attack that completes at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
, and suppose that
\mathcal{S}
has scale
w
at time
t_{\mathrm{jump}}
. Since in the definition of a sneaky attack, we have
k<2^{w+1}
, we conclude that
j\leq w
. Then, from Definition
2
, the scale of the jump is at most
w+1
for either party. As a result,

\Delta\ell^{+}(t_{\mathrm{jump}})\leq 2^{w+2}.

On the other hand, by Remark
11
, the size of the diving window for such an attack satisfies

|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|=2^{w-1}.

Therefore we can conclude that

\Delta\ell^{+}(t_{\mathrm{jump}})\leq 16|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|.

Summing up over all of the completed sneaky attacks, we conclude that

\ell^{*}\leq 16\sum_{i=0}^{k}|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive},i}|\leq 16c^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}),

as desired.
∎

Finally, we state our main technical lemma, which roughly says that if Alice and Bob rewind a lot relative to
L^{-}
, then either
\mathrm{BVC}
increases a lot, or a sneaky attack is in progress.

Lemma 31
.

[Main Technical Lemma]
Fix a time
t^{\dagger}
, and suppose that
k_{A}(t^{\dagger})=k_{B}(t^{\dagger})=:k
.
Let
j^{\dagger}=\lfloor\log k\rfloor.

Suppose that
p
is the next available jumpable point, and that the next jumpable scale is
j_{p}
.
Let
w
be such that
p
is a scale-
w
MP for whichever party is deeper at time
t^{\dagger}
. Further let
b
be the divergent point at time
t^{\dagger}
and let
t_{b}
be the time when
b
became the divergent point.
Suppose that

L^{-}(t^{\dagger})<2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}\leq 2^{j_{p}-3},

(6)

recalling from Definition
11
that
c^{*}\geq 3
is the constant from Definition
12
.

Then at least one of the following must occur:

(1)

\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}(t^{\dagger})\geq\frac{0.6}{4}k
; or

(2)

t_{b}
is during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of a block.

(3)

A sneaky attack heading towards
p
is in progress for either Alice or Bob at time
t^{\dagger}
.

As the proof of Lemma
31
is quite long, we defer it to its own section at the end of the paper, Section
6
.

5.3
Potential Function Analysis

In this section we will analyze how the potential function evolves throughout Algorithm
4.2
. We analyze this in two steps. First we study the potential change per iteration and we show that the potential makes progress during most iterations. Next we analyze the potential per block, showing that the big hash computation that happens at the end of the block minimally effects the potential function. Finally, we conclude by presenting upper and lower bounds on the final values of the potential function.

5.3.1
Potential Change Per Iteration

Lemma 32

(Potential function makes progress)
.

There exist constants
C_{1}<C_{2}<\cdots<C_{6}
(from the definition of
\Phi
in (
2
)), and a constant
C^{-}
, so that the following holds.

Suppose that no big hash collision ever occurs throughout the execution of Algorithm
4.2
.
Let
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
, and let
t_{0}
be the time at the beginning of iteration
I
(Line
15
) and let
t_{1}
be the time at the end of iteration
I
(Line
68
).
Then either a sneaky jump occurs in iteration
I
, or the following holds.

1.

Suppose that:

–

The adversary does not introduce a corruption during iteration
I
;

–

Iteration
I
does not have corrupted randomness;

–

There is no small hash collision in iteration
I
.

Then
\Phi(t_{1})-\Phi(t_{0})\geq 1
.

2.

Otherwise,
\Phi(t_{1})-\Phi(t_{0})\geq-C^{-}
.

Proof.

We will prove this statement by analyzing the different phases of an iteration separately.
In Claim
33
, we focus on the Verification and Computation Phases. We show that during these phases, the change in
\Phi
is bounded in magnitude by some constant
C^{-}
. We also show that, if the conditions of Item 1 above are satisfied, then in fact
\Phi
increases by at least one during these phases.

In Claim
34
we focus on the Transition Phase.
We show that, unless a sneaky jump occurred in iteration
I
,
\Phi
cannot decrease during the Transition Phase.

The two claims together will prove the lemma.

Before we begin, we re-state the definition of the potential function
\Phi
for the reader’s convenience:

\Phi=\begin{cases}\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}+C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{5}E_{AB}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}=k_{B}\\
\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}k_{AB}+C_{4}E_{AB}-C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}\neq k_{B}\end{cases}

We begin with the Verification and Computation Phases.

Claim 33

(Potential progress in Verification and Computation Phases)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Lemma
32
. Let
t_{2}
be the time that the computation phase ends. Then:

1.

If there is neither an adversarial corruption to randomness or communication nor a small hash collision in iteration
I
, then
\Phi(t_{2})-\Phi(t_{0})\geq 1
.

2.

Otherwise,
\Phi(t_{2})-\Phi(t_{0})\geq-C^{-}
for some fixed constant
C^{-}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof for each variable
var
, we will use
\Delta\texttt{var}
to denote the change in
var
during the Verification and Computation Phases. (Note that we will overload this notation in the proof of the next claim, when we focus on the transition phase.)
That is, for this claim,
\Delta(\texttt{var}):=\texttt{var}(t_{2})-\texttt{var}(t_{0})
.

First we observe that each term in
\Phi
changes by at most a constant during the Verification and Computation phases. Indeed,
\ell^{+}
can at most increase by one and cannot decrease at all;
k
could get reset to zero at the end of the Computation Phase, but this line only executes if
k=1
, so
k
can change by at most
1
; and
E
may increase by
1
during the Verification Phase, and may get set to
0
at the end of the Computation Phase, but that only executes if
E=0
. As for the negatively appearing terms,
\ell^{-}
and
L^{-}
can both increase by at most
1
, and
\mathrm{BVC}
does not change. This implies that
\Delta\Phi\geq-C^{-}
, for some constant
C^{-}
, no matter what.

Now suppose that the hypotheses of Item 1 are satisfied; we will show that
\Delta\Phi\geq 1
.
First we notice that, as there are no hash collisions, corruptions, or corrupted randomness in iteration
I
, by
Lemma
10
,

\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
cannot increase.
We break down the analysis into three cases.

•

Case 1: Simulation proceeds.

In this case, we consider the setting where at least one of Alice and Bob simulated
\Pi
during the computation phase. Recall that we are assuming there are no small hash collisions, corruptions, or corrupted randomness. In this case, we claim that
\ell^{-}(t_{0})=\ell^{-}(t_{2})=0
and further,
both
Alice and Bob simulate
\Pi
in the computation phase.

First, we show that
\ell^{-}(t_{0})=0
. Let
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A}(t_{0}))
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B}(t_{0}))
. Suppose toward a contradiction that
\ell^{-}(t_{0})>0
, then by the definition of
\ell^{-}
we know that either
\mathcal{T}_{A}(t_{0})\neq\mathcal{T}_{B}(t_{0})
or
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
. From Corollary
15
we know that in both cases,
\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}
. Then Corollary
16
implies that there exists a variable
\mathtt{var}\in\{\mathtt{p.v},(,\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter})\}
such that
\mathtt{var}_{A}\neq\mathtt{var}_{B}
.

This variable is hashed with a small hash during the verification phase. Since the simulation proceeded for at least one of Alice or Bob, for that party, after the verification phase, we had that

H_{\mathtt{var}}=H^{\prime}_{\mathtt{var}}

(7)

in order to pass the check in line
43
. As we are assuming that there are no corruptions or corrupted randomness, this means that there must have been a small hash collision. However this contradicts our initial assumption that there are no small hash collisions during this iteration. Thus we can conclude that
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{p}_{B}
and as a result
\ell^{-}=0
.

Given that
\ell^{-}(t_{0})=0
, now we show that both parties will simulate during the computation phase.
In order to simulate, per Algorithm
4.2
the following conditions must be satisfied at Line
43
:

–

The small-hashed value of
\mathtt{p.v}
and
(\mathtt{p.T},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.iter})
needs to line up with the hash sent by the other party

–

k=1

–

E=0

–

Rew = False

Without loss of generality, assume that Alice simulated during this iteration. Hence,
E_{A}=0
, indicating that
H_{k_{A}}=H^{\prime}_{k_{B}}
. Since there were no corrupted communications or randomness, and no small hash collisions, it must be that
k_{A}=k_{B}=1
during the verification phase. Indeed, otherwise Alice’s copy of
E_{A}
would have incremented and would not be zero. Given that
k_{B}=1
during the verification phase, we can infer that Bob’s parameters were reset to zero during the previous iteration in line
51
or line
58
of Algorithm
4.2
. As a result,
E_{B}=0
for Bob as well because, as there are no corruptions in this iteration,
H_{k_{B}}=H^{\prime}_{k_{A}}
. Given that

H_{(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter}).A}=H_{(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter}).B}

and that there were no corruptions during iteration
I
, we have that

H_{(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter}).A}^{\prime}=H_{(\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}},\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}},\mathtt{p.T},\mathtt{p.iter}).B},

so Bob also meets the requirements for simulating an iteration. Hence, both Alice and Bob simulate during this iteration. As there are no corruptions during this iteration we have
\mathcal{T}_{A}(t_{2})=\mathcal{T}_{B}(t_{2})
. Further, since there is no corrupted randomness during this iteration, we can conclude that
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{2})=\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{2})
.

Thus, we conclude that
\ell^{-}(t_{2})=0
(and
L^{-}(t_{2})=0
) and both Alice and Bob simulated
\Pi
in the Computation Phase of iteration
I
. As noted above,
\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\leq 0
.
As
k
is reset to zero at the end of the computation phase, we also have
k_{A}(t_{2})=k_{B}(t_{2})=0
; and as noted above
k_{A}(t_{0})=k_{B}(t_{0})=0
. Thus,
\Delta k=0
.

Similarly,
\Delta E=0
. Finally,
\Delta\ell^{+}=1
, as both Alice and Bob correctly simulated the next round. Altogether, we see that
\Delta\Phi\geq 1
in this case.

•

Case 2: Simulation doesn’t proceed and
k_{A}=k_{B}
.
Now, we focus on the case when neither Alice nor Bob simulated
\Pi
in the Computation Phase, but
k_{A}=k_{B}
at the beginning of iteration
I
.
As Alice and Bob do not change their location in
\Pi
during the Verification and Simulation Phase of this iteration, then parameters depending on the location of Alice and Bob, such as
\ell^{-},\ell^{+},L^{-}
, do not change. Since there are no hash collisions or corruptions during this iteration,
\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}=0
, and, as
k_{A}=k_{B}
then
\Delta E_{AB}=0
.
Thus, the only parameter changing throughout
[t_{0},t_{2}]
is
k_{AB}
, which increases by 2. As a result, for
C_{1}\geq 0.5
we get that
\Delta\Phi=C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}\geq 1
.

•

Simulation doesn’t proceed and
k_{A}\neq k_{B}
.
As above, parameters depending on Alice and Bob’s location in the protocol do not change. Also,
\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}=0
.
Then the only parameters changing throughout
[t_{0},t_{2}]
are
k_{AB}
and
E_{AB}
. While
k_{AB}
increases by two,
E_{AB}
also increases by two, as a result the change in the potential equals,
\Delta\Phi=2(1-0.9)C_{4}=0.2C_{4}
. Thus assuming
C_{4}\geq 5
, the change in the potential will be at least
1
.

∎

Next, we analyze the transition phase.
During the transition phase, if a transition happens due to the parameter
E
exceeding the limit
k/2
, then we refer to this transition as an
error transition
; other cases will be referred to as
meeting point transitions
.

Claim 34

(Potential progress in the transition phase)
.

Suppose no big hash collision ever occurs throughout the execution of algorithm
4.2
.

Let
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
, Let
t_{2}
be the end of the computation phase of iteration
I
, as in Claim
33
, and recall that
t_{1}
is the end of the iteration
I
(aka, the end of the transition phase). Then
\Delta\Phi=\Phi(t_{1})-\Phi(t_{2})
has one of the following behaviors:

1.

\Delta\Phi\geq 0
.

2.

One error transition occurred,
k_{A}(I)\neq k_{B}(I)
, and further either
k_{A}(I)=1
or
k_{B}(I)=1
. Then
\Delta\Phi\geq-C^{-}
. Further, assuming that there are no small hash collisions or corruptions in iteration
I
, we have
\Phi(t_{1})-\Phi(t_{0})\geq 1
.

3.

A sneaky attack has occurred. (In this case,
\Delta\Phi
may not be bounded by a constant. This will be analyzed separately.)

Proof.

Overloading notation from the previous claim,
\Delta\texttt{var}
now denotes the change in the value of a variable
var
before and after the
transition
phase.
If no transitions occur during the transition phase, then no parameter within the potential function is changed, resulting in
\Delta\Phi=0
, which proves our claim. However, if there is at least one transition, several scenarios are possible for the transition phase, depending on whether
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
are equal or how many and what type of transition has occurred. We will examine each scenario individually, demonstrating that if there are no sneaky attack jumps during this transition phase, then
\Delta\Phi
will always be non-negative. There is a special sub-case (corresponding to Item 2 in the Claim) where the potential will drop during the transition phase, but overall, during the full iteration, the potential will still increase by one.

(a)

k_{A}\neq k_{B}
and one transition occurred.

Without loss of generality assume that Alice makes the transition. We treat two cases.

Case 1.

Alice makes an error transition.
Then Alice’s and Bob’s locations in the original protocol don’t change, so consequently parameters
\ell^{+},\ell^{-},L^{-}
– which depend on the location of the parties – remain unchanged during this time window. Hence the only parameters that could change are
k_{AB}
,
E_{AB}
, and
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
. Notice that during the iteration
I-1
,
E_{A}(I-1)\leq 0.5(k_{A}(I-1)=0.5(k_{A}(I)-1)
, and during each iteration
E_{A}
can only increase by one, thus during iteration
I
,
E_{A}(I)\leq 0.5(k_{A}(I)-1)+1=0.5k_{A}(I)+0.5
. Now summing up the difference among the parameters according to the potential formula,

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=C_{4}(\Delta E_{AB}(I)-0.9\Delta k_{AB}(I))-C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}(I)

\displaystyle\geq C_{4}(\Delta E_{AB}(I)-0.9\Delta k_{AB}(I))

\displaystyle\geq C_{4}(-0.5k_{A}(I)-0.5+0.9k_{A}(I))

\displaystyle=C_{4}(0.4k_{A}(I)-0.5).

Subcase 1.1:
k_{A}(I)>1
.
In this case from the above we clearly have
\Delta\Phi\geq C_{4}(0.4k_{A}(I)-0.5)\geq 0
.

Subcase 1.2:
k_{A}(I)=1
.

If
k_{A}(I)=1
, then according to the above calculation, the potential difference may be negative during the transition phase. As a result, we will analyze this case for the full iteration and directly prove the statement of Lemma
32
. Consequently, for this subcase, we use
\Delta\mathtt{var}
to refer to the change in a variable over the
entire
iteration, not just the transition phase.
If
k_{A}(I)=1
, then Alice has reset her parameters in the previous iteration; hence, at the beginning of iteration
I
,
k_{A}=0
, and at the end of iteration
I
,
k_{A}=0
again. Thus,
\Delta k_{A}(I)=0
.
Similarly, we have
\Delta E_{A}=0
. Moreover,
\Delta\ell^{-}_{AB}\leq 1
and
\Delta L^{-}_{AB}\leq 1.
This is because we are in Situation (a), so only Bob is continuing simulation; hence
\ell^{-}_{A}
and
L^{-}_{A}
do not change.

Now we prove the conclusion of Lemma
32
in the case that there may be hash collisions or corruptions or corrupted randomness.
During the computation and verification phase, according to Claim
33
,
\Delta\Phi\geq-C
for some constant
C
. As there are no parameter changes for Bob during the transition phase, parameter changes during the transition phase are caused by Alice. Consequently, with
k_{A}=1
then the inequalities
E_{A}\leq 0.5k_{A}+0.5
and
\mathrm{BVC}_{A}\leq k_{A}
show that the negative overall potential change is still bounded by a constant. Thus, the conclusion of Lemma
32
still holds in this case.

Next, we prove the conclusion of Lemma
32
when there are no hash collisions, corruptions, or corrupted randomness during iteration
I
. In this case, notice that
\Delta E_{AB}=\Delta E_{A}+\Delta E_{B}=1
. Indeed, we have
\Delta E_{A}=0
as noted above.
Moreover,
E_{B}
will increase by one: in case (a),
k_{A}\neq k_{B}
, and if there are no hash collisions, corruptions, or corrupted randomness, this means that Bob’s copies of
H_{k}
and
H_{k}^{\prime}
will disagree on Line
34
, and
E_{B}
will be incremented.

Finally, adding up the terms in the potential function we get that

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{AB}+C_{4}\Delta E_{AB}-C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{AB}+C_{4}\Delta E_{AB}

\displaystyle=-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}(\Delta k_{A}+\Delta k_{B})+C_{4}\ (\Delta E_{A}+\Delta E_{B})

\displaystyle\geq-C_{3}-C_{2}-0.9C_{4}+C_{4}

\displaystyle=-C_{3}-C_{2}+0.1C_{4}\ .

This guarantees that
\Phi
will increase by at least one over the course of the whole iteration
I
, for a large enough choice of
C_{4}
.

Case 2.

Alice made a meeting point transition.
Abbreviate
j_{A}=j
, so
k_{A}=2^{j+1}-1
. Observe that the length of Alice’s jump is at most
4k
. This limitation exists because in each jump, the parties consider meeting points at scales
j
and
j+1
, and
\mathrm{MP3}
is at a higher depth than
\mathrm{MP2}
. This is because, according to Definition
2
,
\mathrm{MP3}
is the deepest point
p
such that
2^{j}|p
. Since
2^{j}
divides
\mathrm{MP2}
, then
\mathrm{MP3}
must be at a higher depth. Therefore, the largest jump occurs when Alice decides to jump to
\mathrm{MP1}
, where
\Delta\ell_{A}\leq 2^{j+2}-1\leq 4k_{A}
. As a result, all parameters dependent on Alice’s location will change by a maximum of
4k_{A}
. Specifically, we have
\Delta\ell^{+}\leq 4k_{A}
,
\Delta\ell^{-}\leq 4k_{A}
, and
\Delta L^{-}\leq 4k_{A}
. Additionally, as Alice made a meeting point transition,
\Delta E_{AB}=-E_{A}
and
E_{A}\leq 0.5k_{A}
. Finally, since Alice is the only party transitioning,
\Delta k_{AB}=-k_{A}
. Summing up the terms according to the potential function, we find that

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{A}+C_{4}\Delta E_{A}-C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{A}+C_{4}\Delta E_{A}

\displaystyle\geq-4k_{A}-4(C_{3}+C_{2})k_{A}+0.9C_{4}k_{A}-C_{4}E_{A}

\displaystyle\geq-4k_{A}-4(C_{3}+C_{2})k_{A}+0.9C_{4}k_{A}-0.5C_{4}k_{A}

\displaystyle=k_{A}(0.4C_{4}-4(1+C_{3}+C_{2}))\ .

For large enough
C_{4}
, this ensures a non-negative change in the potential.

(b)

k_{A}\neq k_{B}
and two transitions happen.

For all types of transitions, from Item (a), Case 2, we know that
|\Delta\ell^{+}|\leq 4k_{AB}
,
|\Delta\ell^{-}|\leq 4k_{AB}
,
|\Delta L^{-}|\leq 4k_{AB}
. Additionally, from Item (a), Case 1, we know that
E_{A}\leq 0.5k_{A}+0.5
and
E_{B}\leq 0.5k_{B}+0.5
. Adding the terms up we see that

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{AB}+C_{4}\Delta E_{AB}-C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\delta L^{-}-0.9C_{4}\Delta k_{AB}+C_{4}\Delta E_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-4k_{AB}-4C_{3}k_{AB}-4C_{2}k_{AB}+0.9C_{4}k_{AB}-C_{4}(0.5k_{AB}+1)

\displaystyle\geq k_{AB}(-4(1+C_{3}+C_{2})+0.9C_{4}-0.5C_{4}-\frac{C_{4}}{k_{AB}})

\displaystyle\geq k_{AB}\left(C_{4}(0.9-0.5-\frac{1}{k_{AB}}\right)-4(1+C_{2}+C_{3})

The term
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
was removed after the first inequality because
\mathrm{BVC}
is reset to zero after any transition. This reset has a positive impact on the potential.

Considering that
k_{A}\neq k_{B}
, it follows that
k_{AB}\geq 3
, which implies
0.9-0.5-\frac{1}{k_{AB}}\geq 0.4-\frac{1}{3}>0.06
.

Then assuming
C_{4}>\frac{4(1+C_{2}+C_{3})}{0.06}
the change in the potential is non-negative.

(c)

k_{A}=k_{B}=k
and at least one error transition happened.
As there are either one or two error transitions, the positions of Alice and Bob remain unchanged during the transition phase. Therefore,
\Delta\ell^{+}=0,\Delta\ell^{-}=0,\Delta L^{-}=0
. Furthermore, since there is at least one error transition, we can conclude that
\Delta E_{AB}\geq 0.5k
and
\Delta k_{AB}\leq 2k
. For the potential change, we observe that

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{1}2k+0.5C_{5}k

\displaystyle=k(-2C_{1}+0.5C_{5})\ .

For large enough
C_{5}
, this results in a non-negative change of potential.

(d)

k_{A}=k_{B}=k
and one meeting point transition happened.
Similar to previous cases we have that
\Delta k_{AB}\leq 2k
,
|\Delta\ell^{+}|\leq 4k_{AB}
,
\Delta\ell^{-}\leq 4k_{AB}
and
\Delta L^{-}\leq 4k_{AB}
. If only one meeting point transition happens there are two possible explanations: Either the meeting point was available to both parties and one party didn’t count enough votes, which implies that there were many bad votes so we will have
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}>0.6k
. Otherwise the meeting point was not available to one of the parties and the other party jumped by over-counting the votes. This causes an increase in
\mathrm{BVC}
of at least
0.4k
, implying
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq 0.4k
. As a result the change in the potential will be dominated by the change in the
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
. Formally:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-4k_{AB}-C_{3}4k_{AB}-C_{2}4k_{AB}-C_{1}2k_{AB}+2C_{6}0.4k_{AB}-C_{5}

\displaystyle\geq k_{AB}(-4(1+C_{3}+C_{2})-2C_{2}+0.8C_{6})-C_{5}

\displaystyle\geq 2k(-4(1+C_{3}+C_{2})-2C_{2}+0.8C_{6})-C_{5}.

Thus, for large enough
C_{6}
, the potential difference will be non-negative. The
C_{5}
carrying over throughout the computation is due to the fact that the
E
value for the party not making the transition may increase by
1
during this iteration. The party making a transition will reset the corresponding
E
value to zero, which only has positive effect in the potential change.

(e)

k_{A}=k_{B}=k
and two meeting point transitions happened.

Similar to previous items,
|\Delta\ell^{+}|\leq 4k_{AB}
,
\Delta\ell^{-}\leq 4k_{AB}
and
\Delta L^{-}\leq 4k_{AB}
. Also
E_{AB}
getting reset to zero will only have a positive impact on the potential change.

If
\ell^{-}(t_{1})\neq 0
and Alice and Bob have jumped to points
p_{A}
and
p_{B}
, the mega states corresponding to
p_{A}
and
p_{B}
do not agree. If one of the meeting points is not an available transition candidate for the other party (
p_{A}
for Bob or
p_{B}
for Alice), then
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}>0.4k
because votes counted towards this jump were bad votes. Otherwise,
p_{A}
and
p_{B}
are available to both parties as transition candidates. Without loss of generality, assume that
p_{A}>p_{B}
. During the transition, priority is given to the candidate which is the deeper and has acquired enough votes. Since Bob has not transitioned to
p_{A}
even though it is an available candidate for both parties, the votes for
p_{A}
were under-counted on Bob’s side, implying
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq 0.6k
. Therefore, in both cases, the potential difference will be dominated by
\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}=\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
. Formally:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-4k_{AB}-4C_{3}k_{AB}-4C_{2}-2C_{1}k_{AB}+2C_{6}0.6k

\displaystyle\geq-8k-8C_{3}k-8C_{2}-4C_{1}k+2C_{6}0.6k

\displaystyle=k(1.2C_{6}-8(1+C_{2}+C_{3})-4C_{1})

Hence, for sufficiently large
C_{6}
, the potential change is non-negative.

Now assume that
\ell^{-}(t_{1})=0
. This means that both parties jumped to a common point
p
. Let
j_{p}
be the scale of this jump; note that
j_{p}
was the next jumpable scale before the jump occurred. If
L^{-}\geq 2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}
, we claim that
k_{AB}\leq 2k\leq 2^{c^{*}+2}L^{-}
. This is because
k\leq 2^{j_{p}+1}
as
j_{p}
was the scale of the jump. Then the potential change is dominated by the change in
L^{-}
. Formally, letting
\gamma=2^{c^{*}+2}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq 2\gamma L^{-}+C_{2}L^{-}-2\gamma C_{1}L^{-}

\displaystyle=L^{-}(C_{2}-\gamma(2+C_{1}))

For large enough
C_{2}
, the change in the potential is indeed non-negative.

Otherwise, if
L^{-}<2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}
, then according to Lemma
31
, two cases are possible: either
\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
is large or a sneaky attack jump occurred. The case of sneaky attack jump is treated separately later in our analysis. For now, assume that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\frac{0.6}{4}k
. Then, the potential change is dominated by
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
. Formally:

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi

\displaystyle=\Delta\ell^{+}-C_{3}\Delta\ell^{-}-C_{2}\Delta L^{-}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-C_{5}\Delta E_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\ell^{+}+C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}-2C_{6}\Delta\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}

\displaystyle\geq-8k-8C_{1}k+C_{6}\frac{0.6}{2}k

\displaystyle=k\left(\frac{0.6}{2}C_{6}-8-8C_{1}\right).

As a result for large enough
C_{6}
, the potential difference will be non-negative.

This proves the claim about the behavior of
\Phi
during the transition phase.
∎

Together, Claims
33
and
34
prove Lemma
32
.
∎

Remark 12

(Potential change when a sneaky attack occurs)
.

Lemma
32
shows that the potential function
\Phi
is well-behaved if a sneaky attack does not occur.
Suppose that a sneaky attack jump
does
occur during a transition phrase, completing a sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
. Define
q_{\mathcal{S}}
and
H_{\mathcal{S}}
as in Lemma
25
. Then the proof of Lemma
32
shows that
\Phi
decreases by at most
\Delta|\ell^{+}|+|C_{1}\Delta k_{AB}|\leq\Delta|\ell^{+}|+C_{1}k_{AB}
, as the reset of values in the rest of the terms defining
\Phi
impact the potential change positively.

In Lemma
42
, we will bound the total contribution of this term over all sneaky jumps.

While Lemma
32
says that we may not make progress if small hash collisions occur, in fact we will still make progress in non-dangerous rounds, even if there are hash collisions. We record this fact in the following lemma.

Lemma 35
.

Let
I
be a non-dangerous iteration, so that
I
does not have any corruptions or corrupted randomness. Then
\Phi
increases by
1
over the course of iteration
I
.

Proof.

We begin with a claim that says that, under the conditions of the lemma, we only have certain types of small hash collisions.

Claim 36
.

Given the assumptions of the lemma, small hash collisions only occur between variables related to meeting point candidates. That is, small hash collisions can only occur on lines
29
,
30
,
31
corresponding to the second point in the definition of a small hash collision (Definition
6
).

Proof.

Since
I
is non-dangerous, we have that
k_{A}=k_{B}=1
at the beginning of iteration
I
, and also that
\ell^{-}=0
, which implies that
\mathtt{p}_{A}=\mathtt{p}_{B}
at the beginning of iteration
I
. As a result, all of the variables
var
in line
22
are the same for Alice and Bob:
\mathtt{var}_{A}=\mathtt{var}_{B}
. Thus, it is not possible to have a hash collision on line
23
. This proves the claim.
∎

Now, notice that transitions do not occur in non-dangerous iterations. However, the transition phase is the only time that small hash collisions related to meeting point candidates might matter. Thus, Claim
36
implies that hash collisions do not affect the execution of
\Pi^{\prime}
during iteration
I
. As a result, we may assume that there are no hash collisions at all, in which case the analysis of Lemma
32
applies, and we conclude that
\Phi
increases by one over the course of iteration
I
, as desired.
∎

5.3.2
Potential Change Per Block

One challenge that we have to deal with in the analysis is that there are two cases in which the potential function could decrease by more than a constant. The more challenging case is that of a sneaky attack, which is analyzed in Lemma
30
. Another case that we must deal with is the case that the adversary introduced enough corruptions to interfere with the randomness exchange performed in Line
70
. This second case is much easier to account for. Intuitively, we first observe that as the randomness is protected by an error-correcting code, if the adversary causes a decoding error in the randomness exchange then it must have invested a large number of corruptions. This implies that there are not too many blocks with corrupted randomness, as we make formal in the following lemma.

Lemma 37
.

Let
B_{\mathrm{corrupted}}
be the number of blocks in which Alice or Bob have different seeds for the big hash computation and let
Q
be the total number of corruptions so far. There are at most
Q/(2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}})
such blocks.

Proof.

By Theorem
6
, the binary error-correcting code encoding the bit string
R
has minimum distance at least
4I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
. Thus, for decoding to fail, at least
2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
corruptions must be introduced by the adversary. As the adversary has introduced
Q
corruptions so far, we have

B_{\mathrm{corrupted}}\leq\frac{Q}{2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}\ .

∎

Next, we show that the damage caused by each such block is not too large.

Lemma 38
.

Fix a block
B
and suppose the randomness exchange of Line
70

R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is corrupted. Then the potential function
\Phi
decreases by at most
3I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of Block
B
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
,
k_{AB}
and
E_{AB}
do not change during the Big Hash Computation phase. However, if
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is corrupted, then Alice and Bob will in general not have matching
mega-states
for the depths
p
that were simulated in this block – parameters such as
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
,
\mathtt{p.iter}
and
\mathtt{p.T}
can now differ between the parties, as they were retro-actively updated at the end of the block based on
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
. Thus,
\ell^{+}
can decrease by at most
I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
, while
\ell^{-}
and
L^{-}
can increase by at most
I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
as well. It therefore follows that the total potential decrease is at most
3I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
, as claimed.
∎

Finally we argue that we do not have to worry about the potential function changing during the Big Hash Computation phase in blocks where
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted.

Lemma 39
.

Let
B
be a block such that
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted. Then during the Big Hash Computation phase, the potential function
\Phi
does not change.

Proof.

During the big hash computation variables
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB},E_{AB}
and
k_{AB}
do not change. The following claim will allow us to establish that the variables
\ell^{+},\ell^{-}
and
L^{-}
do not change.

Claim 40
.

Let
t_{0}
be right before the start of big hash computation in line
69
and let
t_{1}
be the end of the block. Further, let
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}
be any two megastates from Alice and Bob’s memory. Then
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{1})=\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{1})
if and only if
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{0})=\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{0})
.

Proof.

If
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{0})=\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{0})
, then
\mathtt{p.iter}_{A}=\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}
at time
t_{0}
. As a result, either the both megastates are simulated during the block
B
—in which case both Alice and Bob use the same variables as inputs to the big hash function, implying their outputs will be the same—or both megastates were simulated in a different block, in which case none of the variables within the megastates are updated during the big hash computation. Hence, we have that
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{1})=\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{1})
.
If
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{0})\neq\mathtt{p}_{B}(t_{0})
then Claim
11
implies
\mathtt{p}_{A}(t_{1})\neq\mathtt{p}(t_{1})
.
∎

The above claim implies
\ell^{+}
and
\ell^{-}
do not change, which also implies
L^{-}
does not change. Thus
\Phi(t_{1})-\Phi(t_{0})=0
.
∎

5.3.3
Upper Bound on
\Phi

In this section, we provide an upper bound on the potential function
\Phi
. This is presented in Lemma
41
below.

Lemma 41

(Upper bound on
\Phi
)
.

Suppose that the constants
C_{2},C_{6}
from (
2
) are sufficiently large, in terms of
C_{1}
from (
2
), and
c^{*}
from Lemma
31
.
Suppose that there are no big hash collisions. Fix
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}].
Let
D=D(I)
be the number of dangerous iterations between iteration
1
and iteration
I
. Then

\Phi(I)\leq\ell^{+}(I)+c_{u}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})\leq I-D+c_{u}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}),

for some constant
c_{u}
, where
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
is the number of dangerous iterations with small hash collisions throughout the execution of Algorithm
4.2
.

Proof.

Recall from (
2
) that

\Phi=\begin{cases}\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}+C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{5}E_{AB}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}=k_{B}\\
\ell^{+}-C_{3}\ell^{-}-C_{2}L^{-}-0.9C_{4}k_{AB}+C_{4}E_{AB}-C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}&\text{if }k_{A}\neq k_{B}\end{cases}

Here, we consider
\Phi(I)
, which we recall is the value of
\Phi
calculated at the
end
of iteration
I
. Below, we drop the “
I
” from the notation, and just note that all variables are calculated at the end of iteration
I
.
Let
D=D(I)
denote the total number of dangerous rounds up to iteration
I
.

We establish the lemma in each of two cases.

Case A.
k_{A}\neq k_{B}
.

In this case, we have

\Phi\leq\ell^{+}+C_{4}(E_{AB}-0.9k_{AB}),

by removing terms that are always negative.
Note that
\ell^{+}\leq I-D
. Indeed, the only way that
\ell^{+}
can increase is if
\ell^{-}=0
and
k_{A}=k_{B}=1
, because Alice and Bob must both simulate rounds of
\Pi
(executing Line
44
), which requires
k_{A}=k_{B}=1
; and because they must have
\mathcal{T}_{A}=\mathcal{T}_{B}
, which requires
\ell^{-}=0
.

Thus, it suffices to show that

E_{AB}\leq 0.9k_{AB}.

(8)

Indeed, then we would conclude that in this case

\Phi\leq\ell^{+}\leq I-D,

which would prove the lemma. To see (
8
), notice that in
I
, either Line
58
was executed by a party (say, Alice) or it was not. If it was executed by Alice, then
E_{A}=k_{A}=0
at the end of iteration
I
. If not, then
2E_{A}<k_{A}
. In either case, we have
E_{A}\leq k_{A}/2
, which is enough to establish (
8
).

Case B.
k_{A}=k_{B}
.

In this case, we can bound

\Phi\leq\ell^{+}-C_{2}L^{-}+C_{1}k_{AB}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB},

where again the inequality holds as we have dropped only negative terms from
\Phi
.
As before, we have
\ell^{+}\leq I-D
, so we wish to bound

C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{2}L^{-}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}.

(9)

To establish (
9
),
we first recall the result of our main technical Lemma,
Lemma
31
.

Let
p
be the next available jumpable point and
j_{p}
be next jumpable scale. Lemma
31
says that three cases are possible. Either:

•

The assumption of Lemma
31
does not hold: That is,
L^{-}\geq 2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}
.

•

The assumption of Lemma
31
holds, and we have outcome
(1)
:
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\frac{0.6}{4}k
, which implies
k_{AB}\leq 16\cdot\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
.

•

The assumption of the Lemma
31
holds, and we have outcome
(2)
: a sneaky attack is in progress.

We will analyze each case separately.

Case B.1:

If
L^{-}\geq 2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}
, then
k_{AB}<2k<\leq 2^{c^{*}+2}L^{-}
. This is because
k\leq 2^{j_{p}+}
given that
j_{p}
is the next jumpable scale. Selecting
C_{2}\geq 2^{c^{*}+2}C_{1}
we get that,

C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{2}L^{-}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\leq 0.

(10)

Case B.2:

If
k_{AB}\leq 16\cdot\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
, by choosing
C_{6}>16C_{1}
large enough, we have that
2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq C_{1}k_{AB}
and hence

C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{2}L^{-}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\leq 0

(11)

Case B.3:
A sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
is in progress.
In this scenario
k_{AB}
may not be on the same scale as other parameters in the potential function. However, by Lemma
25
,
we have

k_{AB}\leq\alpha(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}}),

for some constant
\alpha
, where
Q_{\mathcal{S}}
is the number iterations with corrupted randomness or communication during the diving window of sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
, and
H_{\mathcal{S}}
is the number of dangerous iterations with small hash collisions that occur during the diving window of
\mathcal{S}
.

As a result, we can bound

C_{1}k_{AB}-C_{2}L^{-}-2C_{6}\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\leq C_{1}\alpha(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}})\leq C_{1}\alpha(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}).

(12)

Setting
c_{u}=C_{1}\alpha
, altogether we have that

\Phi(I)\leq\ell^{+}+c_{u}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})\leq I-D+c_{u}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})

∎

5.3.4
Lower Bound on
\Phi

Before we prove our lower bound on
\Phi
, we establish a bound on the total potential decrease caused by sneaky jumps and the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of each block.
Recall from Remark
12
that the potential decrease during an iteration which includes a sneaky jump is at most
|\Delta\ell^{+}(I)|+C_{1}k_{AB}(I)
. In Lemma
42
below, we bound this quantity.

Lemma 42
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{SJ}
be the set of iterations where a sneaky jump occurs. Then the total decrease in
\Phi
over all the iterations in
\mathcal{I}_{SJ}
is bounded by

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}\max\{-\Delta\Phi(I),0\}\leq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}\bigl(|\Delta\ell^{+}(I)|+C_{1}k_{AB}(I)\bigr)\leq C^{\prime\prime}(Q+HC^{d}_{s}),

where
C_{1}
is the constant from (
2
) and
C^{\prime\prime}
is an absolute constant.

Proof.

The first inequality follows from Remark
12
. To establish the second inequality,
recall from Lemma
30
that the total rewind length
\ell^{\ast}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}|\Delta\ell^{+}(I)|
satisfies
\ell^{*}\leq C(Q+HC^{d}_{s})
and further
\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive},i}|\leq C^{\prime}(Q+HC^{d}_{s})
.
Observe that if a sneaky jump is at scale
w
, then
k_{AB}\leq 2^{w+4}
; indeed, the definition of a sneaky attack implies that
k_{A}=k_{B}\leq 2^{w+3}
.
Further, according to Remark
11
,
|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|\geq 2^{w-1}
. Then we have that
k_{AB}\leq 32|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|
. This implies

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}(|\Delta\ell^{+}(I)|+C_{1}k_{AB}(I))

\displaystyle=\ell^{\ast}+C_{1}\Sigma_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}k_{AB}(I)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{*}+32C_{1}\Sigma_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{SJ}}|\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{dive}}|

\displaystyle\leq C(Q+\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s})+32C^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s})

\displaystyle=C^{\prime\prime}(Q+HC^{d}_{s}),

defining
C^{\prime\prime}
appropriately.
This proves the lemma.
∎

Now we bound the total potential decrease caused by Big Hash Computation phases at the end of each block.

Lemma 43
.

The total potential decrease accrued during the Big Hash Computation phases at the end of all blocks is at most
3/2Q
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
39
, there are no potential decreases during the Big Hash Computation phase if
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
is not corrupted. As a result, we only need to count the number of blocks with corrupted
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
, and sum of the potential losses during the big hash computations. Lemma
37
states that there are at most
Q/(2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}})
blocks with corrupted randomness and further Lemma
38
shows that the potential decrease during each one of such blocks is at most
3I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}
. Hence, the total potential decrease caused by big hash computation is
\frac{Q}{2I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}\times 3I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=\frac{3}{2}Q
, which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 44

(Lower bound on
\Phi
)
.

Let
\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s}
be the number of iterations with small hash collisions during dangerous iterations.
Let

S
be the total number sneaky attack jumps and let
Q
be the total number of iterations having either corrupted randomness or corrupted communication. Furthermore
suppose that there are no big hash collisions.
Then for any iteration
I\in[I_{\mathrm{total}}]
, we have

\Phi(I)\geq I-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-O(\varepsilon d)

for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}
, where
C^{-}
is the constant from Lemma
32
.

Proof.

In order to compute this lower bound we will look at the amount of potential accumulated or lost during each iteration prior to our current iteration.
First we look at dangerous iterations. Let
D
denote the number of dangerous iterations.
If no hash collision, corruption, or sneaky jump occurs, (which is the case in at least
D-\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-Q-S
iterations), then by Lemma
32
, the potential function
\Phi
will increase by at least one.
If a hash collision or corruption occurs, then again by Lemma
32
, the potential will decrease by at most a constant
C^{-}
.
Finally, over all of the dangerous iterations, sneaky jumps overall can decrease the potential by at most
C^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})
for some absolute constant
C^{\prime}
, by Lemma
42
. Further, notice that the number of sneaky attacks
S
is also upper bounded by
C^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})
for some constant
C^{\prime}
(which without loss of generality is the same constant, by taking the smaller of the two to be larger), since the number of sneaky attacks cannot exceed the total rewind length caused by all sneaky attacks, which is bounded by a constant times
(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})
by Lemma
30
.

Then if we restrict the potential to only the potential accumulated during dangerous iterations we get

\displaystyle\Phi_{|\text{Dangerous}}

\displaystyle\geq(D-\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-Q)-S-C^{-}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})-C^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})

\displaystyle\geq D-(\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}+Q)-C^{-}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})-2C^{\prime}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})

\displaystyle=D-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})

Next we focus on the
I-D
non-dangerous iterations. Sneaky attack jumps only occur during dangerous iterations as making a jumping transition is not possible during non-dangerous iterations. Thus, we only experience corruptions and small hash collisions in non-dangerous iterations.

By Lemma
35
, if there are no corruptions, then
\Phi
increases by
1
over the course of iteration
I
, regardless of small hash collisions.

On the other hand, if there are corruptions, then the potential function
\Phi
may decrease by at most
C^{-}
, as per Lemma
32
.

Consequently, using the same reasoning as for the dangerous iterations above, the potential accumulated in these non-dangerous iterations is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\Phi_{|\text{Not Dangerous}}

\displaystyle\geq I-D-Q-C^{-}Q

\displaystyle\geq I-D-(C^{-}+1)Q

Finally we need to account for the potential lost during the Big Hash Computation phase of each block. According Lemma
43
, the total potential lost during these phases is at most
3Q/2
.
Adding the three parts together we get that,

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\Phi_{|\text{Dangerous}}+\Phi_{|\text{Not Dangerous}}-\frac{3}{2}Q

\displaystyle\geq I-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})-\left(C^{-}+1+\tfrac{3}{2}\right)Q

Taking into account Lemma
19
, which shows that
Q
is at most
O(\varepsilon d)
,
we can conclude that,

\Phi\geq I-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-O(\varepsilon d),

as desired.
∎

5.4
Hash Function Analysis

Lemma 45

(Small hash collisions)
.

Fix a constant
C^{\prime}
, and suppose that the constant
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
(chosen in
Initialization
, Algorithm
4
) is sufficiently large compared to
C^{\prime}
.
There is a constant
C
so that the following holds.
Suppose that the total number of dangerous iterations during the execution of Algorithm
4.2
is
D
, and suppose that
D\geq C\cdot d\cdot\varepsilon
. Let
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
be the number of dangerous iterations with small hash collisions. Then

\Pr[\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s}\geq D/C^{\prime}]\leq\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}},

where
C_{\delta}
is the constant chosen in Line
8
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{D}
be the number of dangerous iterations without corrupted randomness. Notice that
\hat{D}\geq D-\varepsilon d\geq(1-1/C)D
, as at most
\varepsilon d
iterations can have corrupted randomness and we are assuming
D\geq C\varepsilon d
. As dangerous hash collisions can only occur in dangerous iterations where Alice and Bob are using the same randomness, we restrict our attention to those.

We recall from Algorithm
4
(
Initialize
) that the small hash function is given by

h_{s}(x,R_{1},R_{2})=h_{2}(h_{1}(x,R_{1}),R_{2}),

where:

•

h_{1}
has input length
t_{1}=\log s+O(r\log d+{\log^{2}d})
, output length
o_{1}=2\log(1/\varepsilon)
, and seed length
\mathrm{sd}_{1}=2\cdot t_{1}\cdot o_{1}
(as required for Theorem
5
).

•

h_{2}
has input length
t_{2}=2\log(1/\varepsilon)
, output length
o_{2}=C_{\mathrm{hash}}
, for a constant
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
that we will specify later, and seed length
\mathrm{sd}_{2}=O(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))
(as required for Theorem
4
).

We first analyze the number of hash collisions caused by
h_{1}
.

Claim 46
.

The number of hash collisions in
h_{1}
is at most
D/(2C^{\prime})
with probability at least
1-2d^{-C_{\delta}}
, where
C_{\delta}
is the constant chosen in line
8
.

Proof.

Since the seed for
h_{1}
is shared at the beginning of a block, the adversary is able to tailor their corruptions to the seed for that block. Thus, our approach will be to argue that for any
fixed
pattern of at most
\varepsilon d
corruptions over the
r\cdot I_{\mathrm{total}}=\Theta(d)
rounds of
\Pi^{\prime}
, the conclusion holds with very high probability, and then union bound over all possible patterns of corruptions.

With that in mind, fix a pattern of corruptions. Fix a particular variable
\mathtt{var}
that is hashed by the small hash function. For an iteration
I
, let

Z(I)=\mathbf{1}[h_{1}(\texttt{var}_{A}(I))=h_{1}(\texttt{var}_{B}(I)]\ .

By Theorem
5
, within a given block
B
, the random variables
\{Z(I)\}
appearing in the dangerous iterations in that block (with uncorrupted randomness) are
\delta
-close to a collection of fully independent Bernoulli-
\varepsilon^{2}
random variables
W(I)
, using the fact that
2^{-o_{1}}=2^{-2\log(1/\varepsilon)}=\varepsilon^{2}
. Between the blocks, these random variables are independent.

If instead of the
Z(I)
, we consider the
W(I)
, we see that:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\sum_{I}W(I)\geq D/C^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{D\choose D/(2C^{\prime})}\varepsilon^{2D/(2C^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq(eC^{\prime})^{D/(2C^{\prime})}\varepsilon^{2D/(2C^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq(eC^{\prime}\varepsilon)^{2Cd\varepsilon/(2C^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=(eC^{\prime}\varepsilon)^{Cd\varepsilon/C^{\prime}},

(13)

where above we used the fact that for any
b\leq a
,
{a\choose b}\leq(ea/b)^{b}
.

Now we can union bound over all of the ways for the adversary to distribute the corruptions, as well as over the
O(1)
variables that are hashed with the small hash function.
There are at most

{r\cdot I_{\mathrm{total}}\choose\varepsilon d}\leq{2d\choose\varepsilon d}\leq\left(\frac{2e}{\varepsilon}\right)^{d\varepsilon}

(14)

ways for the adversary to distribute corruptions. Thus, by choosing the constant
C
large enough relative to
C^{\prime}
in (
13
), the probability that there exists
any
corruption pattern that causes
\sum_{I}W(I)
to be larger than
D/(2C^{\prime})
is at most

6\cdot\left(\frac{2e}{\varepsilon}\right)^{d\varepsilon}\cdot(eC^{\prime}\varepsilon)^{Cd\varepsilon/C^{\prime}}\leq\varepsilon^{\Omega(d\varepsilon)}.

Finally, we recall that the
W(I)
are
\delta
-close to the
Z(I)
, for
\delta=2^{-C_{\delta}I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}=d^{-C_{\delta}}
. This implies that

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\sum_{I}Z(I)\geq D/C^{\prime}\right\}\leq\varepsilon^{\Omega(d\varepsilon)}+d^{-C_{\delta}}.

Since
C_{\delta}
is a constant, the second term is much larger than the first and dominates the expression. This proves the claim.
∎

Next, we analyze the collisions arising from
h_{2}
.

Claim 47
.

Provided that the constant
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
is sufficiently large (in terms of
C^{\prime}
), the number of hash collisions in
h_{2}
is at most
D/(2C^{\prime})
with probability at least
2^{-\Omega(d\varepsilon)}
.

Proof.

Fortunately, as this randomness is shared right before it is used, the adversary cannot adapt their corruptions to influence the objects being hashed. Thus, we may treat the values that
h_{2}
is hashing as fixed in our analysis.

As in the proof of Claim
46
, fix a variable
var
to consider, and let

Z(I)=\mathbf{1}[h_{2}(\texttt{var}_{A}(I))=h_{2}(\texttt{var}_{B}(I)].

Now the
Z(I)
are fully independent Bernoulli-
p
random variables, where
p=2^{-C_{\mathrm{hash}}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}
, using Theorem
4
and the choice of
o_{2}=C_{\mathrm{hash}}
. (Recall that
{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}
is the constant in Theorem
4
.) Thus,

\mathbb{E}\sum_{I}Z(I)=\hat{D}p\geq(1-1/C)Dp.

Thus, if we choose
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
large enough (in terms of
C,C^{\prime}
, and
{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}
) so that

(1-1/C)2^{-C_{\mathrm{hash}}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}\leq\frac{1}{4C^{\prime}},

a Chernoff bound implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\sum_{I}Z(I)\geq D/(2C^{\prime})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\sum_{I}Z(I)\geq 2\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{I}Z(I)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\Omega(D))

\displaystyle=\exp(-\Omega(d\varepsilon)).

Finally, a union bound over the
O(1)
possibilities for
var
establishes the claim.
∎

Finally, if a small hash collision occurs, then a hash collision occurs in either
h_{1}
or
h_{2}
, and furthermore, the number of dangerous iterations containing a small hash collision is at most the total number of small hash collisions during dangerous iterations. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 48

(Number of Dangerous iterations)
.

For any protocol
\Pi
, let
D
be the total number of dangerous iterations. Then
D=O(\varepsilon d)
with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be a sufficiently large constant. To prove the lemma, we will show that if
D\geq\alpha\varepsilon d
, then the number of dangerous iterations with small hash collisions
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
is larger than
\frac{D}{C^{\prime}}
, where
C^{\prime}
is the constant from Lemma
45
. As Lemma
45
shows that the probability of having so many hash collisions is at most
\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
, the desired claim would follow.

So assume
D\geq\alpha\varepsilon d
. By Lemma
41
and the fact that
q\leq 2\varepsilon d
we get,

\Phi\leq I-D+c_{u}(\varepsilon d+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}).

Next, by Lemma
44
,

\Phi\geq I-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-\Theta(\varepsilon d).

Combining these two inequalities gives:

\displaystyle I-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-\Theta(\varepsilon d)

\displaystyle\leq I-D+c_{u}(\varepsilon d+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})

\displaystyle-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1+c_{u})\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-\Theta(\varepsilon d)

\displaystyle\leq-D

\displaystyle\frac{D-\Theta(\varepsilon d)}{C^{-}-2C^{\prime}+1+c_{u}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}

By choosing
\alpha
large enough, assuming
D\geq\alpha\varepsilon d
will imply that the numerator above is at least
D/2
, which implies that

\frac{D}{2(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1+c)}\leq\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}.

(15)

Recall that Lemma
45
says that for any
\alpha^{\prime}
(which controls the constant
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
), there is some
C
so that if
D\geq Cd\varepsilon
, then the probability that
\mathsf{HC}_{s}\geq D/\alpha^{\prime}
is small.
18
18

18

We note that in that lemma,
\alpha^{\prime}
is called
C^{\prime}
; we avoid that notation here as we already have
C^{\prime}
defined in this scope.

Thus, we choose
\alpha^{\prime}\geq 2(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1+c)
, and set
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
appropriately large; then we set
\alpha\geq C
large enough that the lemma applies, and conclude that

\Pr[\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}\geq D/\alpha^{\prime}]\leq\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}.

But this implies that the probability that (
15
) occurs is small. Since (
15
) follows from the assumption the
D\geq\alpha\varepsilon d
, this in turn implies that

\Pr[D\geq\alpha\varepsilon d]\leq\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}},

completing the proof.

∎

Corollary 49
.

The number of dangerous small hash collisions,
\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s}
, satisfies
\mathsf{HC}^{d}_{s}=O(\varepsilon d)
with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
48
and the fact that the number of dangerous small hash collisions—which by definition (Definition
8
) can only occur during dangerous iterations—is bounded above by a constant (namely, the number of variables hashed with the small hash function) times the number of dangerous iterations.
∎

Corollary 50

(
k
is not too large during a sneaky attack)
.

Let
I
be an iteration during the voting window of a sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
. Then
k_{A}(I)=k_{B}(I)=k
with
k=O(\varepsilon d)
with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
24
we have
k\leq\alpha(Q_{\mathcal{S}}+H_{\mathcal{S}})
, where we recall that
H_{\mathcal{S}}
is the number dangerous iterations having small hash collisions during the sneaky attack
\mathcal{S}
. Since
H_{\mathcal{S}}\leq\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
,
Q\leq 2\varepsilon d
and further from Lemma
48
we get that
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
is at most
O(\varepsilon d)
with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
, from which it follows that
k\leq\alpha(\varepsilon d+O(\varepsilon d))=O(\varepsilon d)
, proving the statement.

∎

Corollary 51

(The total rewind during all sneaky attacks is bounded.)
.

Following the notation of Lemma
30
, define
\ell^{\ast}
as the total rewind length of all sneaky attacks during the execution of Algorithm
4.2
. Then
\ell^{\ast}\leq O(\varepsilon d)
with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
30
,
\ell^{\ast}\leq 4c(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d})
, where
q
is the total number of corruptions during the protocol execution,
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
is the total number of small hash collisions, and
c
is some constant. We now plug in the upper bound
Q\leq\varepsilon d
(which is the adversary’s corruption budget) and the upper bound
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}\leq O(\varepsilon d)
(which holds with probability
\geq 1-\frac{2}{d^{C_{\delta}}}
by Corollary
49
) to derive the corollary.
∎

Having now shown that the number of dangerous small hash collisions will be small (i.e.,
O(\varepsilon d)
) with high probability, we now turn to bounding the number of big hash collisions. The following lemma argues that we will in fact have
zero
big hash collisions with high probability.

Lemma 52

(Big hash collisions)
.

With probability at least
1-d^{-10}
, no big hash collisions occur during the entire protocol.

Proof.

The seed for the hash function
h^{b}
is shared immediately before the big hash computation phase; thus, similarly to the proof of Claim
47
, the adversary’s corruptions that influence the objects being hashed do not depend on this randomness. As a result, we may treat the values that are hashed by
h^{b}
as being fixed in our analysis.

Consider a block
B
, and let
x_{\mathtt{p}}
be the inputs to the big hash function
h^{b}
in line
74
for the
\mathtt{p}
simulated within this block. Further, define

\displaystyle Z(B)=\mathbf{1}[\exists\mathtt{p}_{A},\mathtt{p}_{B}\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle h^{b}(x_{\mathtt{p}_{A}},R_{A}(B))=h^{b}(x_{\mathtt{p}_{B}},R_{B}(B))

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathtt{p}_{A}\neq\mathtt{p}_{B},

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathtt{p.iter}_{A},\mathtt{p.iter}_{B}\in B],

where
R_{A}(B),R_{B}(B)
is the randomness used by Alice and Bob in the hash. (Note that here,
B
in the subscript refers to Bob, while
B
elsewhere is the block).
That is,
Z(B)
is one if there are two megastates
\mathtt{p}_{A}
and
\mathtt{p}_{B}
that Alice and Bob simulated in the block
B
that are not the same but so that the corresponding big hashes match.

Notice that as we are counting the number of big hash collisions we may restrict our attention to those iterations where Alice and Bob have the same randomness; thus we assume that
R_{A}(B)=R_{B}(B)=R(B)
. Then,

\Pr[Z(B)=1]\leq\log^{2}d\times\frac{1}{d^{C_{b}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}},

(16)

where
{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}
is the constant in Theorem
4
.
Indeed, by Theorem
4
, and our choice of
o_{3}=C_{b}\log d
, the probability of a hash collision in
h^{b}
is at most
2^{-o_{3}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}=d^{-C_{b}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}.
Then union bounding over the at most
\log(d)
mega-states per block for each party yields (
16
).

Finally, we union bound over all
B_{\mathrm{total}}
blocks. We have

B_{\mathrm{total}}=\left\lceil I_{\mathrm{total}}/I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{\lceil d/r\rceil+\Theta(d\varepsilon)\rceil}{\lceil\log d\rceil}\right\rceil\leq\frac{2d}{\log d}

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. Thus, the probability that a big hash collision occurs at any point in the simulation is at most

\frac{2d}{\log d}\times\log^{2}d\times\frac{1}{d^{C_{b}/{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}}}\leq d^{-10}

by choosing
C_{b}
sufficiently large in terms of
{K}_{\mathrm{hash}},
and for sufficiently large
d
.

∎

Recall that Assumption
1
asserted that for any two distinct blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
, the randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}(B)
used in
B
and
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}(B^{\prime})
used in
B^{\prime}
were distinct. Now we remove this assumption by showing that it holds with high probability.

Lemma 53
.

Let
B
and
B^{\prime}
be two distinct blocks during the execution of Algorithm
4.2
. Let
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)
and
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})
denote the randomness used by Alice during block
B
and by Bob during block
B^{\prime}
respectively. Then the probability that
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})
is at most
d^{-13}
.

Proof.

Each party creates their copy of the seed
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
by concatenating two smaller seeds,
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b,1}
generated by Alice in Line
70
, and
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b,2}
generated by Bob in Line
71
. As a result, for Alice the first half of
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}
is uncorrupted randomness; similarly for Bob the second half of
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}
is uncorrupted randomness.

Assume without loss of generality that
B^{\prime}>B
. As the randomness for each block is generated independently, we may treat
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)
as an arbitrary fixed string of length
2\ell
relative to the randomness generated during block
B^{\prime}
. As noted above, the second half of
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}
is uncorrupted randomness of length
\ell
.

\displaystyle\Pr[(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b,2})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b,2})_{B}(B^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=2^{-\ell}.

Recall that we set
\ell=\mathrm{sd}_{3}={K}_{\mathrm{hash}}(C_{b}\log d+\log 1/\varepsilon)
(in line
23
of Algorithm
4
). Thus, by choosing
C_{b}
sufficiently large in terms of
{K}_{\mathrm{hash}}
, we have that

\Pr[(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})]\leq d^{-13},

proving our claim.
∎

Lemma 54

(Unique Big Hash Randomness)
.

During the running time of Algorithm
4.2
, the probability that there exist two distinct blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
such that
(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})
is at most
\frac{1}{d^{10}}
.

Proof.

We will prove this statement by taking a union bound over all possible pairs of
B
and
B^{\prime}
. According to Lemma
53
, for any two distinct blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}
,

P[(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{A}(B)=(R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b})_{B}(B^{\prime})]\leq d^{-13}.

Note that
B_{\mathrm{total}}=\lceil I_{\mathrm{total}}/I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}\rceil\leq d+\theta(\varepsilon d)\leq 2d
for large enough
d
, where the first equality is how we chose
B_{\mathrm{total}}
in Line
8
of Algorithm
4
. Hence, the total number of possible pairs
(B,B^{\prime})
of distinct blocks is at most
B_{\mathrm{total}}^{2}\leq 4d^{2}
. Thus the probability of finding such pair is at most
4d^{2}\times d^{-13}\leq d^{-10}
for large enough
d
, proving our claim.
∎

5.5
Proof of Theorem
1

In this section, we prove Theorem
1
.
We restate the theorem for the reader’s convenience.

See
1

Proof.

We first recall a few parameters from the protocol, for the reader’s convenience:

I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=\lceil\log d\rceil\ ,\ I_{\mathrm{total}}=\lceil d/r\rceil+\Theta(d\varepsilon)\ ,\ B_{\mathrm{total}}=\lceil I_{\mathrm{total}}/I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}\rceil.

(17)

We prove the theorem in the following four steps. We first outline all four, and then go through each of them.

1.

First, we establish that the simulation is correct, assuming no big hash collisions occur, and that the number of dangerous iterations is
O(\varepsilon d)
. To establish this, we use the lower bound on the potential function (Lemma
44
), we show that if no big hash collision occurs during the simulation and the number of dangerous iterations throughout the simulation is small then the potential function grows. By tying this potential increase to the length of the correct simulation using the upper bound on the potential function (Lemma
41
), we will show that
\ell^{+}\geq d/r
, which guarantees that by the end of
I_{\mathrm{total}}
iterations the algorithm has correctly simulated
\Pi
.

2.

We next bound the probability of failure, which by the above is bounded by the probability that there were any big hash collisions, or that there were too many dangerous iterations.

3.

Next, we work out the parameters to analyze the memory usage of
\Pi^{\prime}
.

4.

Next, we work out the parameters to analyze the rate.

5.

Finally, we bound the running time of
\Pi^{\prime}
.

We go through each of these below.

1.

Correctness of simulation.

The upper bound on potential function in Lemma
41
tells us that, in any iteration
I
, and in particular iteration
I=I_{\mathrm{total}}
,

\Phi(I_{\mathrm{total}})\leq\ell^{+}(I_{\mathrm{total}})+c_{u}(Q+\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}),

assuming that no big hash collision occurs during the entire protocol. Next, we plug in bounds for
Q
and
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
.
Assuming that the number of dangerous iterations is
O(\varepsilon d)
, Corollary
49
states that
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}
, the number of dangerous small hash collisions, is at most
O(\varepsilon d)
. Since
Q
, the number of corruptions introduced throughout
\Pi
’, is at most
2\varepsilon d
, we conclude that

\Phi(I_{\mathrm{total}})\leq\ell^{+}(I_{\mathrm{total}})+c_{u}(\varepsilon d+\Theta(\varepsilon d))=\ell^{+}(I)+\tilde{c_{u}}\varepsilon d,

for some constant
\tilde{c_{u}}.

Now we use the lower bound on the potential from Lemma
44
.
Lemma
44
implies that, at the end of the algorithm,

\Phi(I_{\mathrm{total}})\geq I_{\mathrm{total}}-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}-\Theta(\varepsilon d),

and using

q\leq 2\varepsilon d
and
\mathsf{HC}_{s}^{d}=\Theta(\varepsilon d)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi(I_{\mathrm{total}})

\displaystyle\geq I_{\mathrm{total}}-(C^{-}+2C^{\prime}+1)\Theta(\varepsilon d)-\Theta(\varepsilon d)\geq I_{\mathrm{total}}-c_{l}\varepsilon d

for some constant
c_{l}
.

Comparing the two, we see that

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{total}}-(c_{l}+\tilde{c_{u}})\varepsilon d\leq\ell^{+}(I_{\mathrm{total}}).

Thus, choosing the total number of iterations large enough, so that

I_{\mathrm{total}}\geq\lceil d/r\rceil+2(c_{l}+\tilde{c_{u}})\varepsilon d,

we have

\ell^{+}(I_{\mathrm{total}})\geq\lceil d/r\rceil.

By definition, this means that when Algorithm
4.2
terminates at iteration
I_{\mathrm{total}}
, the length of the correct simulated path is at least
\lceil d/r\rceil
, which is in fact the entire (iteration) length of
\Pi
(recall Remark
8
). We conclude that Alice and Bob have correctly simulated all of
\Pi
, as desired.

2.

Probability of failure.

Item 1 shows that if there are no big hash collisions in the entire protocol and there are
O(\varepsilon d)
dangerous iterations, Alice and Bob can correctly simulate the protocol
\Pi
.

For the protocol to fail, there are three possibilities. First, it could be that the Assumption
1
does not hold, which, according to Lemma
54
, happens with probability at most
d^{-10}
. Second, there could be a big hash collision during the protocol, which, according to Lemma
52
, happens with probability at most
1/d^{10}
. Third, the protocol must have more than
\Theta(\varepsilon d)
dangerous iterations, which, according to Lemma
48
, occurs with probability at most
2/d^{C_{\delta}}
. Here,
C_{\delta}>1
is an arbitrary constant from Line
8
of Algorithm
4.2
. If we choose it to be, say,
C_{\delta}=2
, the total probability of failure is
2/d^{10}+2/d^{C_{\delta}}\leq 1/\mathrm{poly}(d)
.

3.

Memory
. Next we compute the memory that each party uses during Algorithm
4.2
.
Each party stores:

•

A set
\mathtt{AvMPs}
with size at most
O(\log d)
containing available meeting points. Each point is an integer in the range between
[0,d]
, thus requires at most
\log(d)
bits for representation. In total,
\mathtt{AvMPs}
will need
\Theta(\log^{2}d)
bits in memory.

•

A memory
\mathtt{Memory}
, containing mega-states corresponding to each
p\in\mathtt{AvMPs}
. Each mega state contains:

–

\mathtt{p.v}
: A state with
\log(s)
bits

–

\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-hash}}
: A hash function with
o_{3}=O(C_{b}\log d)
bits

–

\operatorname{\mathtt{p.prev-seed}}
: A seed for
h^{b}
with
\mathrm{sd}_{3}=O(\log d+\log 1/\varepsilon)
bits.

–

\mathtt{p.iter}
and
\mathtt{p.depth}
: integers of size at most
d
, and hence which take at most
\log d
bits.

–

\mathtt{p.T}
: We recall that
\mathtt{p.T}
is a placeholder variable, referencing the partial simulated path
T
, and not actually stored. Thus, we do not need to account for any space it takes up.

Thus, each megastate uses
\log(s)+O(\log d)
bits, and all
O(\log d)
of them together require

O(\log(s)\log(d)+\log^{2}d)
bits.

•

The memory to store the single copy of
T
that each
\mathtt{p.T}
points to; this is
O(r\log d+\log^{2}d)
bits.

•

The parameters and state variables for the current iteration/block:
k,E,j,v,I_{\mathrm{current}},I_{\mathrm{cnt}}
. All of these except
v
require
O(\log d)
bits, and
v
requires
\log s
bits.

•

The hash values output and received from both the big and small hash function. Outputs of the small hash function have size
o_{2}=O(1)
, and Alice and Bob must also compute the intermediate values of
h_{1}
, which have size
O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
.
The big hash function has outputs of size
o_{3}=O(C_{b}\log d)
.

•

The randomness generated and exchanged. For
h_{2}
, the randomness
R_{\mathrm{iter}}
is generated in each iteration, exchanged, and then forgotten; this is
\mathrm{sd}_{2}=O(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))
bits to store.

The randomness
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}
is a bit trickier, and for this we need to deviate a bit from the stated pseudocode, as per the comment after Line
8
.

Instead of storing all of
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}
, Alice and Bob will instead store only
\tilde{R}_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}
, and generate the blocks
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}[I]
that they need on the fly in each iteration
I
. To verify that we can do this, we need to open up the pseudorandom
extend
function of Theorem
5
, from
[
NN90
]
. The way that this works is as follows. Alice and Bob agree on a suitable matrix
G\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2^{\ell^{\prime}}\times\ell}
, which is the generator matrix for a
\delta
-balanced error correcting code of rate
\mathrm{poly}(\delta)
. Then the
extend
function uses the seed of length
\ell^{\prime}
to select a row of this matrix, and then outputs it. In particular, if the generator matrix is explicit, Alice and Bob can generate any entry of it—and in particular each bit in the short chunk needed for
R_{\mathrm{block}}[I]
—on the fly. There exist such explicit matrices: for example, an AG code concatenated with a Hadamard code will work (see page 2 of
[
BATS09
]
). Thus, Alice and Bob need only store
\tilde{R}_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}
.

For
\tilde{R}_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}
, the length of this is the value of
\ell^{\prime}=O(\log\ell+\log(1/\delta))
in Theorem
5
used in the construction of the small hash.
We recall that
\ell\leftarrow I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}\mathrm{sd}_{1}
and
\delta\leftarrow 1/\mathrm{poly}(d)
, where

\mathrm{sd}_{1}=2o_{1}t_{1}=O(\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(s)+r\log(1/\varepsilon)\log(d)+\log(1/\varepsilon)\log^{2}d).

Thus,
\ell^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}=O(\log(\ell)+\log(1/\delta))

\displaystyle=O(\log d+\log\log s+\log r+\log\log d)

\displaystyle=O(\log d+\log\log s+\log r)

\displaystyle=O(\log d+\log\log s)=O(\log d),

where we have used that
r=\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)
and
\varepsilon>1/d
(or else the adversary cannot introduce any corruptions); and that
s\leq 2^{d}
.

Alice and Bob also need the seed chunks
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{s}[I]
during each iteration; however, as discussed above, they can generate these one bit at a time on the fly, and so we do not need to account for any extra memory usage.

Finally, during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of each block, Alice and Bob exchange and temporarily store
R_{\mathrm{block}}^{b}
, which contains

\mathrm{sd}_{3}=O(\log(d)+\log(1/\varepsilon))=O(\log d)

bits by Claim
7
.

Adding up all the requirements, and recalling that
r=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\right)
, the dominating terms are

\displaystyle O\left(\log(s)\log(d)+\log^{2}(d)+r\log(d)\right)=O\left(\log(s)\log(d)+\sqrt{\frac{\log\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}\log d\right)

Above, we used that
d\leq s
. Thus, when
\varepsilon
is constant this is
O(\log(d)\log(s))
, as desired.

4.

Rate.

In the simulation of the Algorithm
4.2
we have
B_{\mathrm{total}}
blocks such that

I_{\mathrm{total}}=B_{\mathrm{total}}I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=\lceil d/r\rceil+\Theta(\varepsilon d).

During each block,
O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))
bits of communication are used in order to exchange randomness for the small hash at the start of the block.
Indeed, the length of seed transmitted is the parameter
\ell^{\prime}
in Algorithm
4.2
. We have

\ell^{\prime}=O(\log(1/\delta)+\log(\ell)),

where
\delta=2^{-C_{\delta}I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}}
and

\ell=\mathrm{sd}_{1}\cdot I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=2(\log s+O(r\log d))\cdot 2\log(1/\varepsilon)\cdot I_{\mathrm{perBlock}},

plugging in the parameters for
\mathrm{sd}_{1}
from Algorithm
4
.
Thus, the dominant terms in
\ell^{\prime}
are
\log(1/\delta)=C_{\delta}I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}=O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}})
and the
O(\log\log(s))
and
O(\log r)=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
terms that appear in
\log(\ell)
. Since
O(\log\log(s))=O(\log d)=O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}})
, we see that
\ell^{\prime}=O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))
as claimed. Now, the number of bits transmitted is
O(\ell^{\prime})
, as this seed is encoded with a constant-rate error correcting code.

Next, we account for the randomness exchange for the big hash at the end of the block. From the proof of Claim
7
, we know that the seed length for
h^{b}
is
\mathrm{sd}_{3}=O(C_{b}\log d+\log(1/\varepsilon))=O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))
. This is communicated after being encoded with a constant rate error correcting code, so the total number of bits communicated is still
O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))
.

Other than that, in each iteration, we have
r
bits of communication to simulate the
r
rounds of
\Pi
; and we have the overhead from exchanging randomness for the small hash, and to exchange the hashes themselves. The output of each small hash has length
C_{\mathrm{hash}}
, which is constant, so this is dominated by
\mathrm{sd}_{2}
, the length of the seed for
h_{2}
, which is
O(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))
. Let
r_{c}
be the amount of extra communication in each iteration, so
r_{c}=O(\log\log(1/\varepsilon))
, and the total amount of communication in each iteration (not including the randomness exchange at the beginning of each block) is at most
