r+r_{c}
bits.

Thus, for each block, there are a total of
O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))+I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}(r+r_{c})
bits of communication. As a result, in the whole algorithm, the total amount of communication, in bits, is at most

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{total}}\cdot(O(I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}+\log(1/\varepsilon))+I_{\mathrm{perBlock}}(r+r_{c}))

\displaystyle\qquad=O(I_{\mathrm{total}})+O(B_{\mathrm{total}}\log(1/\varepsilon))+I_{\mathrm{total}}(r+r_{c}).

Now, we have (ignoring floors and ceilings for clarity)

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{total}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d/r+O(d\varepsilon)}{\log(d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{d/r}{\log d}+O(d\varepsilon/\log d)

\displaystyle=\frac{d\sqrt{\varepsilon/\log\log(1/\varepsilon)}}{\log d}+O\left(\frac{d\varepsilon}{\log d}\right).

Returning to the above, the term
O(B_{\mathrm{total}}\log(1/\varepsilon))
is thus
O(d\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, using the fact that
\varepsilon\geq 1/d
(or else there would be no errors) and hence
\log(d)\geq\log(1/\varepsilon)
.

Thus, the total amount of communication is

\displaystyle O(I_{\mathrm{total}})+O(d\sqrt{\varepsilon})+I_{\mathrm{total}}(r+r_{c})

\displaystyle=O(d\sqrt{\varepsilon})+\left(\lceil d/r\rceil+O(d\varepsilon)\right)(O(1)+r+r_{c})

\displaystyle=O(d\sqrt{\varepsilon})+\left(d+O(d\varepsilon r)\right)\cdot\left(O(1/r)+1+\frac{r_{c}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=d\left[O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})+(1+\varepsilon r)\cdot\left(1+\frac{r_{c}}{r}+O(1/r)\right)\right].

By plugging in
r=\lceil\sqrt{\frac{r_{c}}{\varepsilon}}\rceil
, and
r_{c}=\Theta(\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
, we see that the total amount of communication is

d\left(1+O\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon\log\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\right)\right).

5.

Time.
We compute the time overheads of
\Pi^{\prime}
relative to
\Pi
. The block-level pseudorandomness exchanges have
\mathrm{poly}(\log d,1/\varepsilon)
output length, and therefore yield a
\mathrm{poly}(\log d,1/\varepsilon)
overhead. For the hash function computations, again the logarithmic lengths on the inputs and outputs imply a
\mathrm{poly}(\log d,\log s,1/\varepsilon)
bound on the overhead. Maintaining the available meeting points sets also incur a
\mathrm{poly}(\log d\cdot\log s)
overhead.
The steps that just require reading and writing from memory take time at most
\mathrm{poly}(\log s,\log d)
. Thus, per iteration, the total amount of overhead is
\mathrm{poly}(\log s,\log d,1/\varepsilon)
. The number of iterations is
I_{\mathrm{total}}=O(d)
, so the total contribution of the above are
d\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log d,\log s,1/\varepsilon)
.

Finally, there is simulating the original protocol
\Pi
itself. The number of extraneous iterations of
\Pi
that are simulated is
\Theta(\varepsilon d)
, with
r
rounds of communication in each. If we conservatively imagine that all of the time it takes to execute
\Pi
is contained in these extraneous simulations, it incurs a multiplicative overhead of
\mathrm{poly}(d).
Thus, the total running time of
\Pi^{\prime}
is at most the running time
T
of
\Pi
, times a
\mathrm{poly}(d/\varepsilon)
factor.

Altogether, the total running time is
d\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log d,\log s,1/\varepsilon)+T\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d/\varepsilon)=T\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d/\varepsilon)
, as claimed. Above, we have used the fact that
T\geq d
, because the running time of
\Pi
is at least its depth.

This completes the proof of all five items, and thus of our main theorem.
∎

6
Proof of Main Technical Lemma

We restate Lemma
31
for the reader’s convenience:

See
31

Proof.

Let
\ell_{A}=\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})
and
\ell_{B}=\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})
. Throughout the proof we assume that
t_{b}
is not during the Big Hash Computation phase at the end of a block. (That is, we assume that Item 2 does not hold). Thus, our goal is to show that, under this assumption, either Item 1 or Item 3 holds. All claims are considered for time
t^{\dagger}
unless stated otherwise.

Claim 55
.

At time
t^{\dagger}
, either
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})\cup\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B})
or
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
.

Proof.

Throughout, let

\gamma=\frac{0.6}{4}.

Assume that
p\notin\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})\cup\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B})
. Then the fact that
p
is the next available jumpable point implies that

p=\mathrm{MP3}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=\mathrm{MP3}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B}).

(18)

We first claim that

p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(\ell_{A},j_{p}-1)\cap\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(\ell_{B},j_{p}-1).

(19)

To see (
19
), notice that for
(
18
) to hold,
it must be that
p\leq\ell_{A},\ell_{B}<p+2^{j_{p}}
.
Now consider
\ell_{A}
; there are two options, either
\ell_{A}<p+2^{j_{p}-1}
or
\ell_{A}\geq p+2^{j_{p}-1}
. In the first case, we have
\mathrm{MP3}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=p
. In the second case, as
p+2^{j_{p}-1}\leq\ell_{A}\leq p+2^{j_{p}}
, then
\mathrm{MP2}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=\lfloor\ell_{A}\rfloor_{2^{j_{p}-1}}-2^{j_{p}-1}=p
.
In either case,
p
is a transition candidate for Alice at scale
j_{p}-1
, that is
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(\ell_{A},j_{p}-1)
.
The same logic also applies to Bob, so
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(\ell_{B},j_{p}-1)
. This establishes (
19
).

Now, we finish the proof of Claim
55
.
If
j_{p}-1\geq j^{\dagger}
, then (
19
) implies that
j_{p}-1
is also a jumpable scale; but this contradicts the fact that
j_{p}
is the next jumpable scale. Thus we have that
j_{p}=j^{\dagger}
. This means that during iterations of scale
j^{\dagger}-1=j_{p}-1
,
p
was an available transition candidate for both parties. Given that Alice and Bob have skipped this scale, then according to Lemma
21
the adversary must have introduced corruptions to cause
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
to increase at least
(0.6)2^{j^{\dagger}-1}
times during the iterations corresponding to scale
2^{j^{\dagger}-1}
. As
k
is at most
2^{j^{\dagger}+1}
, we have that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq(0.6)2^{-2}2^{j^{\dagger}+1}\geq\gamma k
, where
\gamma=(0.6)2^{-2}
. This proves the claim.
∎

For the remainder this proof, assume without loss of generality that
\ell_{A}\geq\ell_{B}
.
Then we have the following claim.

Claim 56
.

Either
\mathrm{MP1}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
or
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}<\gamma k
, so we want to show that
p=\mathrm{MP1}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
. From Claim
55
we know that

p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})\cup\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B}).

Further, because we are assuming without loss of generality that
\ell_{A}\geq\ell_{B}
, in fact we have that
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
: Indeed, if
\mathrm{MP3}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
and
\ell_{B}\leq\ell_{A}
then
\mathrm{MP3}(j_{p},\ell_{B})=p
, which results in the same contradiction as in proof of Claim
55
.
Thus, to show that
p=\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
, it suffices to show that
p\neq\mathrm{MP2}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
.

Assume towards a contradiction that
\mathrm{MP2}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
.
Now we will show that under this assumption, then
BVC_{AB}\geq\gamma k
, a contradiction; to see this we will show that in this case,
p
is an available transition candidate on scale
j_{p}-1
, which means that the adversary had to invest enough corruptions for Alice and Bob to have skipped it.

To that end, first observe that

p=\mathrm{MP2}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=\mathrm{MP1}_{A}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A}),

where the first equality is by our assumption and the second always holds by definition.
Further, the assumption that
\mathrm{MP2}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
, along with the fact that
\ell_{A}\geq\ell_{B}
, implies that
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{2,3\}}(j_{p},\ell_{B})
. In more detail,
because
p
is a transition candidate, it must be one of
\mathrm{MP1},\mathrm{MP2}
or
\mathrm{MP3}
for Bob, so we just need to show that
p\neq\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p},\ell_{B})
. To see this, suppose towards a contradiction
p=\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p},\ell_{B})
; this implies that
|\ell_{B}-p|>2^{j_{p}+1}
. However, since
p=\mathrm{MP2}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
, we have
|\ell_{A}-p|<2^{j_{p}+1}
. Given that
p\leq\ell_{B}\leq\ell_{A}
, this is a contradiction.

Thus, either
p
is
\mathrm{MP2}
or
\mathrm{MP3}
for Bob at scale
j_{p}
. We treat each scenario separately.

1.

\mathrm{MP2}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B})=p
. In this case,
p
is a scale
j_{p}
meeting point for both Alice and Bob. As a result, by the definition of
\mathrm{MP1}
,
\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{B})=p
, meaning that
p
is an available transition candidate for scale
j_{p}-1
.

2.

\mathrm{MP3}_{B}(j_{p},\ell_{B})=p.
In this case,
\ell_{B}\in[p,p+2^{j_{p}})
. Now if
\ell_{B}\in[p+2^{j_{p}-1},p+2^{j_{p}})
then
\mathrm{MP2}_{B}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{B})=p
; otherwise,
\mathrm{MP3}_{B}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{B})=p
. In either case, again
p
is an available transition candidate in scale
j_{p}-1
.

This shows that, if
\mathrm{MP2}_{A}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
, then
p
is an available transition candidate at scale
j_{p}-1
.
Now, if
j_{p}>j^{\dagger}
,
j_{p}-1
should be the next available jumpable scale, but this contradicts the lemma statement. As a result we conclude that
j_{p}=j^{\dagger}
. In this case, the adversary has invested in many corruptions during scale
j^{\dagger}-1=j_{p}-1
, in order to skip the point
p
in this scale. Thus, according to Lemma
21
,
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq(0.6)2^{j^{\dagger}-1}\geq\frac{0.6}{4}k
. This is a contradiction with the assumption that
\mathrm{BVC}<\gamma k
. As a result,
\mathrm{MP2}(j_{p},\ell_{A})\neq p
. Knowing that
p\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}(j_{p},\ell_{A})
we can conclude that
\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p},\ell_{A})=p
, as desired.
∎

Given Claim
55
and Claim
56
, from now on we assume that
\mathrm{MP1}_{A}=p
, and in particular that

j_{p}=w-1.

(20)

As a result, from now on we can also assume that

L^{-}(t^{\dagger})<2^{w-c^{*}}\leq 2^{w-3}.

(21)

We will show that either
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
or a sneaky attack is in progress.

Let
L^{-}=L^{-}(t^{\dagger})
. Now, as
p
is the scale
w
meeting point for Alice, we may write
p=(\alpha-1)2^{w}
for some integer
\alpha
. Let
\hat{p}=p+2^{w}
. Let
b
be the divergent point at time
t^{\dagger}
. Notice that this exists as we assume that
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})>0
.
Let
D=b-p
. Notice that
D>0
, because

2^{w-3}>L^{-}\geq\ell_{A}-b\geq\hat{p}-b,

(22)

where above we have first used (
21
); then the definition of
L^{-}
; then the fact that

\ell_{A}<\hat{p}=p+2^{w},

(23)

which follows since
p
is a scale-
w
MP for Alice.
This implies that

b>\hat{p}-2^{w-3}>p.

(24)

Define
q=p+2^{w-1}=\hat{p}-2^{w-1}
. Notice that
q<b
, since otherwise we have that

2^{w-3}>L^{-}\geq\ell_{A}-b\geq\hat{p}-q=2^{w-1},

which is not true.

Organization.

We break the rest of the proof up into
Item 1
and
Item 2
, depending on whether
b<\hat{p}
or
b\geq\hat{p}
. as follows:

Item 1.

If
b<\hat{p}
, first we will show that
q
is a jumpable point. Then we will show that if
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
or
q\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, then
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
. Otherwise, we will show that a sneaky attack is in progress.

Item 2.

if
b\geq\hat{p}
, or if
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
, then we will show that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
, by showing that
\hat{p}
is an available jumpable point in form of
\mathrm{MP3}
for both parties in scale
j^{\dagger}-1
.

We begin with the proofs regarding
Item 1
, and then we will do
Item 2
(which is much shorter) after that.

Item 1.

We begin with a claim about the point
q
.

Claim 57
.

Suppose that
b<\hat{p}
.
With the set-up above, at time
t^{\dagger}
, all of the following hold:

•

q
is a scale-(
w-1
) MP for Alice.

•

q
is either a scale-(
w-1
) or a scale-
(w-2)
MP for Bob.

•

q
is a jumpable point at scale
w-2
.

Proof.

Notice that, since
p
is a scale-
w
MP for Alice at time
t^{\dagger}
, we have
\ell_{A}\geq\hat{p}
. We also have

2^{w-3}>L^{-}\geq\ell_{A}-b>\ell_{A}-\hat{p},

where the first inequality is by (
21
); the second is the definition of
L^{-}
; and the last is the assumption that
b<\hat{p}
that we are making in Case 1. Thus,
\hat{p}\leq\ell_{A}<\hat{p}+2^{w-3}
, which implies that
q=\lfloor\ell_{A}\rfloor_{2^{w-1}}-2^{w-1}
is a scale-
(w-1)
MP for Alice, and in particular that
q\in M_{\ell_{A}}
.
Next, the same logic shows that
\ell_{B}<\hat{p}+2^{w-3}
, and from the definition of
L^{-}
we also have
\ell_{B}\geq\ell_{A}-L^{-}\geq\hat{p}-2^{w-3}
, so

\hat{p}-2^{w-3}\leq\ell_{B}<\hat{p}+2^{w-3}.

(25)

If
\ell_{B}\in[\hat{p}-2^{w-2},\hat{p})
, then
q
is a scale-
(w-2)
MP for Bob; if
\ell_{B}\in[\hat{p},\hat{p}+2^{w-2}),
the
q
is a scale-
(w-1)
MP for Bob. In either case,
q\in M_{\ell_{B}}
. Now at scale
w-2
,
q=\mathrm{MP1}_{A}(w-2,\ell_{A})
and
q\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2\}}_{B}(w-2,\ell_{B})
thus
q
is a jumpable point at scale
w-2
.
∎

Claim 58
.

At time
t^{\dagger}
, if
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
then
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
.

Proof.

First we have the following sub-Claim.

Sub-Claim 59
.

If
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, then

q
is an available jumpable point at scale
j_{p}-1
.

Proof.

By Claim
57
,
q
is a jumpable point at scale
w-2
. As we are assuming in the statement of the Sub-Claim that
q
is available for both parties,
q
is an available jumpable point at scale
w-2
. We have also established earlier in (
20
) that
j_{p}=w-1
, so
j_{p}-1=w-2
, and the proof is complete.
∎

If
j_{p}-1\geq j^{\dagger}
, it contradicts the assumption in Lemma
31
that
j_{p}
is the next available jumpable scale. So
j_{p}-1<j^{\dagger}
.
Alice and Bob have skipped
q
as a meeting point, which according to Lemma
21
implies that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq(0.6)2^{j_{p}-1}
.
As
j_{p}\geq j^{\dagger}
, it means
j_{p}=j^{\dagger}
and
j^{\dagger}-1=j_{p}-1
, so
BVC_{AB}\geq(0.6)2^{j^{\dagger}-1}\geq\gamma k
where
\gamma=(0.6)2^{-2}
, where in the last inequality we have used the fact that
k<2^{j^{\dagger}+1}
by the definition of
j^{\dagger}:=\lfloor\log k\rfloor
.
This proves the claim.
∎

Claim 60
.

Assume that
b<\hat{p}
. Then, given the above set-up, we have that
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction assume otherwise. This means that Alice and Bob have forgotten the state at the point
q
by reaching a state at depth
c_{q}=q+2^{w}=\hat{p}+2^{w-1}
. Let
t_{c_{q}}^{A}
be the time that Alice reaches
c_{q}
for the last time before
t^{\dagger}
. Let
t_{c_{q}}^{B}
be the time that Bob reaches
c_{q}
for the last time before
t^{\dagger}
. Then we have the following sub-claim.

Sub-Claim 61
.

With the set-up above,
t_{c_{q}}^{A},t_{c_{q}}^{B}<t_{b}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction assume otherwise, and without loss of generality assume
t_{c_{q}}^{A}\geq t_{b}
. Then,
L^{-}(t_{c_{q}}^{A})=c_{q}-b\geq c_{q}-\hat{p}=2^{w-1}
. This contradicts the assumption that
L^{-}\leq 2^{w-3}
. As a result we have that
t_{c_{q}}^{A},t_{c_{q}}^{B}<t_{b}
, as desired.
∎

Let time
\min\{t_{c_{q}}^{A},t_{c_{q}}^{B}\}\leq t\leq t_{b}
be the first time either party jumps above
\hat{p}
after that same party has reached
c_{q}
. Note that such a time
t
must exist because
b<\hat{p}
. This is because, given our assumption that
t_{b}
is not during a Big Hash Computation phase, and applying Lemma
18
, Item 1, we see that some party must jump to
b
to start the bad spell at time
t_{b}
.
Let us first assume that this is Bob.
Then by Lemma
20
, Bob jumps to a point above
q
at time
t
. Since
\ell_{B}\geq b
, Bob passes the point
q
at least one more time before
t^{\dagger}
. As a result, either
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, which contradicts our assumption in the start of the proof; or Bob forgets
q
again at some time after
t_{c_{q}}^{B}
and before
t^{\dagger}
, which contradicts the assumption that
t_{c_{q}}^{B}
was the last time Bob forgot
q
before
t^{\dagger}
. In either case, we have a contradiction of our assumption that
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
.

The same logic applies if Alice is the party who first jumps above
\hat{p}
. Indeed, the only part of the logic above that was specific to Bob is that
\ell_{B}\geq b
; since
\ell_{A}\geq\ell_{B}\geq b
, the same holds for Alice too.

Thus, in either case we have a contradiction of our assumption that
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, so we conclude that indeed
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, which proves the claim.
∎

So far we shown (in Claim
58
) that if
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, then
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
. We’ve also shown (in Claim
60
) that if
b<\hat{p}
, then
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
.

Next, in Claim
62
we will show that, assuming
b<\hat{p}
, if only one party has
q
as an available meeting point (that is, if
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
but
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
) then a sneaky attack must be in progress. This will complete the proofs for
Item 1.

Claim 62
.

Suppose that
b<\hat{p}
.
Suppose that
q
is an available meeting point for exactly one party at time
t^{\dagger}
; that is,
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cup\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
but
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
. Then a sneaky attack is in progress.

Proof.

To prove this statement we will show that all properties of a sneaky attack are satisfied. Consider the case where
q\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
but
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
. The case where
q\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
and
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
follows in a similar manner.

Assuming
q\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
but
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, then at some point before
t^{\dagger}
, Alice has reached a point of depth
c_{q}=q+2^{w}=\hat{p}+2^{w-1}
and forgot
q
.

Definition 15
.

Let
t_{c_{q}}\leq t^{\dagger}
be the last time Alice reaches
c_{q}
. Further define
t_{\hat{p}}
to be the last time Alice reaches
\hat{p}
before
t_{c_{q}}
.

Then we have the following claim.

Sub-Claim 63
.

For any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
,
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that there exists a time
t\in(t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
such that
\ell_{A}(t)<\hat{p}
.

If
t\in(t_{\hat{p}},t_{c_{q}}]
, then there also exists a time
t^{\prime}\in(t,t_{c_{q}}]
such that
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})=\hat{p}
. This is because Alice needs to pass depth
\hat{p}
to reach
c_{q}>\hat{p}
, but this contradicts the definition of
t_{\hat{p}}
.

On the other hand, if
t\in(t_{c_{q}},t^{\dagger}]
, suppose without loss of generality that
t
is the first time after
t_{c_{q}}
so that
\ell_{A}(t)<\hat{p}
.
Then according to Lemma
20
,
\ell_{A}(t)\leq q
. Given that
\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})\geq b
and
b>q
, Alice must pass the depth
q
again to reach
\hat{p}
. This reintroduces
q
into
\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
. If Alice never reaches
c_{q}
again before time
t^{\dagger}
, then
q\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
at time
t^{\dagger}
, but this contradicts the assumption that
q\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
. On the other hand, if Alice
does
reach
c_{q}
again, this would contradict the definition of
t_{c_{q}}
. In either case, by contradiction we reach the conclusion that
\ell_{A}(t)\geq t_{\hat{p}}
for all
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
, as desired.
∎

We will show that
t_{c_{q}}
and
t_{b}
are in two different bad spells using the claims below.

Sub-Claim 64
.

t_{b}>t_{c_{q}}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction assume otherwise, so
t_{b}\leq t_{c_{q}}
. Then the time
t_{c_{q}}\in[t_{b},t^{\dagger})
is included in the bad spell containing
t^{\dagger}
.
But then we have

L^{-}(t^{\dagger})\geq L^{-}(t_{c_{q}})\geq c_{q}-b\geq c_{q}-\hat{p}=2^{w-1}>2^{w-3}.

Above, the first inequality is because
L^{-}
is non-decreasing during a bad spell; the second is because at time
t_{c_{q}}
, Alice is at depth
c_{q}
while the divergent point is
b
, so
L^{-}
is at least
c_{q}-b
; and the third is because
b<\hat{p}
by assumption.
However,
this contradicts the fact that
L^{-}\leq 2^{w-3}
at time
t^{\dagger}
. Thus
t_{b}>t_{c_{q}}
and the proof is complete.
∎

Sub-Claim 65
.

Bob is at the point
b
at time
t_{b}
. That is,
\ell_{B}(t_{b})=b
.

Proof.

As we have assumed that
t_{b}
is not during the Big Hash Computation phase, Lemma
18
Item 1 implies that, for a point
b
to become a divergent point, one of two things must occur. First, it could be that at time
t_{b}-1
,
\ell_{A}=\ell_{B}=b
and
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A})=\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B})
; and then at time
t_{b}
,
\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{A})\neq\mathtt{ms}(\ell_{B})
and
b
becomes the last point Alice and Bob agree on and hence the divergent point. Alternatively, it could be that at time
t_{b}
, a party has jumped to point
b
making it the divergent point.

In the current set-up, the first case is not possible. To see this, notice that
t_{b}>t_{\hat{p}}
and as a result
t_{b}-1\geq t_{\hat{p}}
. From Claim
63
we know that
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}
for any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
thus,
\ell_{A}(t_{b}-1)\neq b
.
Thus, we conclude that the second has occurred, and some party has jumped to
b
at time
t_{b}
.

Again from Claim
63
,
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}>b
for any
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
. Further from Sub-Claim
64
and the definition of
t_{\hat{p}}
, we know that
t_{\hat{p}}\leq t_{c_{q}}<t_{b}\leq t^{\dagger}
, so we cannot have
\ell_{A}(t_{b})=b
. Thus, we reach the conclusion that Bob has made a jump to point
b
at time
t_{b}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Sub-Claim 66
.

For any time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{b}]
,
\ell_{B}(t)<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
.

Proof.

For contradiction assume there exists a time
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{b}]
such that
\ell_{B}(t)\geq\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
. We will show that this contradicts the fact that Bob jumps to point
b
at time
t_{b}
(Claim
65
).

First notice that, if
b
is
j_{b}
stable, then
j_{b}\leq w-c^{*}
. The reason is that
b\in[\hat{p}-2^{w-c^{*}},\hat{p})
,
and
\hat{p}
is at least
w
-stable.
Thus,
\hat{p}-2^{w-c^{*}}
can be at most
(w-c^{*})
-stable;
and anything in the open interval
(\hat{p}-2^{w-c^{*}},\hat{p})
is at most
(w-c^{*})
-stable, as it lies strictly between consecutive multiples of
2^{w-c^{*}}
.
Thus, if Bob reaches depth
\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
(which by our assumption he does at some time in
(t_{\hat{p}},t]
), he will forget the meeting point
b
, because Bob forgets
b
at depth
c_{b}=b+2^{j_{b}+1}=\hat{p}+2^{j_{b}}\leq\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
.

However, by Claim
65
,
\ell_{B}(t_{b})=b
. Then there must be a time
t^{\prime}\in[t,t_{b}]
such that
\ell_{B}(t^{\prime})<b
and
b
is reintroduced as a meeting point afterward. However, from Claim
63
we know that
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})\geq\hat{p}
, so Alice and Bob cannot be at the same depth at time
t^{\prime}
; this implies that
t^{\prime}
is in a bad spell, and further that the divergent point at time
t^{\prime}
is some
b^{\prime}\leq\ell_{B}(t^{\prime})<b
.

This bad spell is only resolved if Alice and Bob simultaneously reach a point above
b^{\prime}
. Thus this bad spell is not resolved by
t^{\dagger}
. But this contradicts the fact that at
t^{\dagger}
,
b
is the divergent point, because
b^{\prime}<b
and the new divergent can only decrease in depth, by to Lemma
18
(Item 6). Hence, such a time
t
does not exist and we can conclude that
\ell_{B}(t)<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
for all
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{b}]
.
∎

Sub-Claim 67
.

The times
t_{c_{q}}
and
t_{b}
are in two different bad spells. That is, there exists a time
t^{\ast}
and a point
\hat{q}
such that
t_{c_{q}}<t^{\ast}<t_{b}
and
\ell_{A}(t^{\ast})=\ell_{B}(t^{\ast})=\hat{q}
and
\ell^{-}=0
. Additionally, we have that
\hat{q}\geq\hat{p}
.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction assume otherwise. Then the bad spell containing
t^{\dagger}
starts at some time
t
and contains both
t_{c_{q}}
and
t_{b}
; so
t_{c_{q}},t_{b}\in[t,t^{\dagger}]
.

Consider the value of
L^{-}(t_{c_{q}})
at time
t_{c_{q}}
.
Using the fact from Sub-Claim
66
that
\ell_{B}(t_{c_{q}})\leq\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
, we have:

\displaystyle L^{-}(t_{c_{q}})

\displaystyle=\ell_{A}(t_{c_{q}})-\ell_{B}(t_{c_{q}})

\displaystyle=c_{q}-\ell_{B}(t_{c_{q}})

\displaystyle\geq\hat{p}+2^{w-1}-(\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}})

\displaystyle=2^{w-1}-2^{w-c^{*}}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{w-2}.

This contradicts the assumption of Lemma
31
that
L^{-}(t^{\dagger})\leq 2^{j_{p}-c^{*}}\leq 2^{w-c^{*}}
, as
L^{-}(t^{\dagger})\geq L^{-}(t_{c_{q}})\geq 2^{w-2}
, and we have
c^{*}\geq 3
. Thus it is not possible for
t_{c_{q}}
and
t_{b}
to be in the same bad spell. We conclude that a
t^{\ast}
exists such that
\ell_{A}(t^{\ast})=\ell_{B}(t^{\ast})=\hat{q}
and
\ell^{-}(t^{\ast})=0
.

Finally, we claim that
\hat{q}\geq\hat{p}
. This follows because by Sub-Claim
63
,
\ell_{A}(t)\geq\hat{p}
for all
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t^{\dagger}]
.
In particular, this holds at time
t^{\ast}
, when
\ell_{A}(t^{\ast})=\hat{q}
.
∎

Sub-Claim 68
.

If
t^{\ast}
is the first time Alice and Bob are ending a bad spell since
t_{c_{q}}
, then
\hat{q}=\hat{p}
,where
\hat{q}
is as in the statement of Sub-Claim
67
.

Proof.

We begin with some intuition.
For Alice and Bob to end a bad spell they must simultaneously reach a point
\hat{q}
such that
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\hat{q})}_{A}=\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\hat{q})}_{B}
and
\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{B}
.
In particular, it is important to note that Alice and Bob do
not
get to re-simulate the point
\hat{q}
that meet at: rather
\hat{q}
was supposed to be a point that was simulated correctly, and Alice and Bob meet up at the
end
of that previous simulation. Informally, we will show that if
\hat{q}\neq\hat{p}
, then the last time that Alice and Bob simulated
\hat{q}
(before they first end a bad spell after
t_{c_{q}}
) was in fact
during
a bad spell, and we show that this implies that they did not simulate
\hat{q}
correctly. Thus meeting up at the end of
\hat{q}
cannot have resolved the bad spell. We make this formal below.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
\hat{q}
is not equal to
\hat{p}
.
Notice that when Alice reaches the point
c_{q}
at time
t_{c_{q}}
, she forgets all meeting points in the interval
(\hat{p},\hat{p}+2^{w-2})
. From Claims
66
and
63
we know that
\hat{q}\in[\hat{p},\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}})
. As a result, if
\hat{q}\neq\hat{p}
, then Alice must have simulated
\hat{q}
at some time
t\in[t_{c_{q}},t^{\ast})
.
Moreover, this time
t
was during the first bad spell. To see this, first note that
t\leq t^{\ast}
, so it was before the first time a bad spell ends after
t_{c_{q}}
. Moreover, we know that by time
t_{c_{q}}
, this first bad spell had already begun, since Alice was at depth
\ell_{A}(t_{c_{q}})=c_{q}
and Bob was not, since by Sub-Claim
66
,
\ell_{B}(t_{c_{q}})<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}\leq c_{q}
.

By definition, since the bad spell ends at the point
\hat{q}
, it must be that
\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\hat{q})}_{A}=\mathcal{T}^{(\leq\hat{q})}_{B}
and
\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{B}
at the time
t^{\ast}
when the bad spell ends.
In particular, Alice and Bob’s simulated transcripts must have agreed on
\hat{q}
at time
t^{\ast}
. Since simulated paths include iteration numbers,
\mathcal{T}_{A}^{(\leq\hat{q})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{B}^{(\leq\hat{q})}
can only have agreed if Alice and Bob simulated
\hat{q}
during the
same
iteration
I
. Note that the iteration
I
contains the time
t
defined above, the time when Alice simulated
\hat{q}
during the first bad spell; in particular,
I
was during a bad spell.

However, Lemma
18
(Item 4), says that if
I
is an iteration during a bad spell, and if both Alice and Bob simulate a point
\hat{q}
during iteration
I
, then after the simulation,

\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{A}\neq\mathtt{ms}(\hat{q})_{B}
.
But this contradicts requirement above that mega-states agree if a bad spell is resolved.

Overall, we get a contradiction, and conclude that
\hat{q}=\hat{p}
. The proof is complete.
∎

Finally, we put everything together to show that a sneaky attack is in progress, which proves the claim. We go through each element of a sneaky attack. In the proof above, we have defined the times
t_{\hat{p}}<t_{c_{q}}<t_{\hat{q}}<t_{b}<t^{\dagger}
, and we check that the requirements of Definition
12
hold.

•

By Definition
15
,
\ell_{A}(t_{c_{q}})=c_{q}
and
t_{c_{q}}
is indeed the last time that this happens before
t^{\dagger}
.

•

By Definition
15
again,
t_{\hat{p}}
is the last time that Alice passes
\hat{p}
before
t_{c_{q}}
.

•

The fact that Alice and Bob jump to a point
\hat{q}\geq\hat{p}
to end a bad spell at time
t_{\hat{q}}
; and that
\hat{q}=\hat{p}
if this is the first time that a bad spell has ended after
t_{c_{q}}
, are established by Sub-Claims
67
and
68
.

•

From the statement of the lemma, the divergent point becomes
b
at time
t_{b}
and
b
remains the divergent point until
t^{\dagger}
. Further, Sub-Claim
65
implies that Bob jumps to
b
at time
t_{b}
. The fact that
b\geq\hat{p}-2^{w-c^{*}}
follows from (
24
).

•

Sub-Claim
66
implies that
\ell_{B}(t^{\prime})<\hat{p}+2^{w-c^{*}}
for all
t\in[t_{\hat{p}},t_{b})
. The fact that
\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})<\hat{p}+2^{w-3}
follows from the fact that

2^{w-3}\geq 2^{w-c^{*}}\geq L^{-}\geq\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})-b>\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})-\hat{p}

•

The fact that
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}(t^{\dagger})
follows from our assumption at the beginning of the proof. (If instead we had assumed that
q\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}(t^{\dagger})
, then the same proof goes through switching the roles of Alice and Bob, and we will conclude that a sneaky attack is in progress, but with Bob rather than Alice as the target.)

∎

This completes the elements of
Item 1
, where we address the case the
b<\hat{p}
.

Item 2.

Next, we consider the elements of

Item 2
, where we consider the case that either
b\geq\hat{p}
or that
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
.

We begin with the case that
b\geq\hat{p}
; as we will see afterwards, the proof follows almost identically if
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
.

We already knew from (
23
) and the fact that
\ell_{B}\leq\ell_{A}
that

\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger}),\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})\leq\hat{p}+2^{w}.

Now with the additional assumption that that
b\geq\hat{p}
and the fact that
\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger}),\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})\geq b
, we have that

\hat{p}\leq\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger}),\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})<\hat{p}+2^{w}.

(26)

As before, define
t_{b}
as the time when
b
becomes the divergent point in the bad spell including
t^{\dagger}
. Define
t_{\hat{p}}^{A}
and
t_{\hat{p}}^{B}
as the last time that the corresponding party has reached
\hat{p}
before time
t_{b}
. Then we have the following claim.

Claim 69
.

If
b\geq\hat{p}
, then
\hat{p}\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
\hat{p}\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}\cap\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
, and suppose first that
\hat{p}\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
. (The case where
\hat{p}\not\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
follows similarly). Let
t_{\hat{p}}\leq t^{\dagger}
be the last time Alice reaches
\hat{p}
before
t^{\dagger}
. (Note that this is
different
than the definition of
t_{\hat{p}}
in Item 1, as it is the last time before
t^{\dagger}
, not before
t_{b}
.)
Note that
2^{w}|\hat{p}
, which implies that if
\hat{p}
is
j
-stable, then
j\geq w
. If
p\notin\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
, then there exists a time such that Alice has forgotten
\hat{p}
by reaching a point at depth
c_{\hat{p}}=\hat{p}+2^{j+1}\geq\hat{p}+2^{w+1}
. Let
t
be the last time Alice reaches
c_{\hat{p}}
. Notice that upon reaching
c_{\hat{p}}
, Alice forgets all meeting points in the interval
(\hat{p},\hat{p}+2^{w})
. However we know from (
26
) that

\hat{p}\leq\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})<\hat{p}+2^{w}.

Then according to Lemma
20
there must be a time
t^{\prime}\in(t,t^{\dagger})
such that Alice jumps to a point above
\hat{p}
,
\ell_{A}(t^{\prime})\leq\hat{p}
. As a result of this Alice must cross the depth
\hat{p}
one more time to reach depth
\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})
; but this contradicts the fact that
t_{\hat{p}}
is the last time before
t^{\dagger}
that Alice has reached
\hat{p}
.
This proves that
\hat{p}\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{A}
. The same logic follows for Bob showing that
\hat{p}\in\mathtt{AvMPs}_{B}
. Thus the proof is complete.
∎

Claim 70
.

If
b\geq\hat{p}
, then at time
t^{\dagger}
,
\hat{p}
is a available jumpable point for scale
j^{\dagger}-1
. Thus
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}\geq\gamma k
.

Proof.

We will first show that
\hat{p}
is an available jumpable point for scale
j_{p}-1
. Since
j_{p}
is the next available jumpable point, thus it must be that
j_{p}-1=j^{\dagger}-1
and we will use Lemma
21
to show that this means that
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
must be large, and the proof will be complete.

Since
j_{p}=w-1
, we have
j_{p}-1=w-2
. At this scale:

•

if
\ell_{A}\in[\hat{p},\hat{p}+2^{w-2})
then
\mathrm{MP3}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=\hat{p}
;

•

if
\ell_{A}\in[\hat{p}+2^{w-2},\hat{p}+2^{w-1})
then
\mathrm{MP2}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=\hat{p}
;

•

if
\ell_{A}\in[\hat{p}+2^{w-1},\hat{p}+2^{w})
then
\mathrm{MP1}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A})=\hat{p}
.

In any case,
\hat{p}\in\mathrm{MP}^{\{1,2,3\}}(j_{p}-1,\ell_{A}),
and
the same logic holds for Bob.
Further, our assumption in Item 2 that
b\geq\hat{p}
implies that
\mathtt{ms}(\hat{p})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\hat{p})_{B}
.
We conclude that
\hat{p}
is indeed a available jumpable point for scale
w-2=j_{p}-1
.

However,
we know that
j_{p}
is the next available jumpable scale. As a result it must be that
j_{p}-1<j^{\dagger}
and by Lemma
21
, the adversary has invested in corruption to create bad vote counts and skip this scale. Then it must be that
j_{p}=j^{\dagger}
. Thus at time
t^{\dagger}
,
BVC_{AB}\geq(0.6)2^{j_{p}-1}=(0.6)2^{j^{\dagger}-1}\geq\gamma k
.
This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

Finally, we address the case that
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
.
In this case, all of the arguments from the case that
b\geq\hat{p}
go through. Indeed, (
26
) holds because now
\ell_{A}(t^{\dagger})=\ell_{B}(t^{\dagger})
; since
p
is a scale-
w
MP for Alice, it is also a scale-
w
MP for Bob, and (
26
) follows.
From this, the proof of Claim
69
follows unchanged. The only part of the proof of Claim
70
that requires
b\geq\hat{p}
(and in particular that
b
exists) is in order to establish that
\mathtt{ms}(\hat{p})_{A}=\mathtt{ms}(\hat{p})_{B}
; but if
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
, then this holds by definition. Thus, the proof of Claim
70
goes through as well.

Finally, we put together the logic in
Item 1
and
Item 2
as described in the
Organization
paragraph above, and conclude that:

1.

If
b<\hat{p}
, then either
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
is large or a sneaky attack is in progress.

2.

If either
b\geq\hat{p}
or if
\ell^{-}(t^{\dagger})=0
, then
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
is large.

In either case, we conclude that either
\mathrm{BVC}_{AB}
is large or a sneaky attack is in progress, which proves Lemma
31
.

∎
